"AA unnecessary formality," said Jack, stooping down ta poke the fire. "Don't let me disturb you," he IsTnt, on crossly. . "You don't," said Andrew, kissing Margaret again, and resting his arm on her shoulder. "If there's one thing on earth which drives me to frenzy," said Jack, "it is to see other people kissing one another! Why can't you go into panther room, if you must kiss? " "Don't mind him, Margaret," said Andrew, laughing heartily. "It's.just his way. He never means what he says." •

"You will excuse me, but you are not to kiss Mdget again in my presence. 1 will not allow it t" said Jack, standing up with his back to the

"Midget ? " inquired Margaret. "It is what /I have christened you," said Jack gravely. "I have also found a name for Andrew, but that is for your ear alone." "Oh, I don't mind," said Andrew. "Every- body knows that Jack is quite mad! But where's mother?

"Mother has undutifully abandoned her children and has gone to spend the day with Mrs. Smuggins, ever the way." _

Margaret laughed, and Andrew looked inquir- ingly at Jack, and asked: "What's the joke? Her name isn't Smuggins; it's Brent. You never remember anybody's name."

"Smuggins is a very good name. Why be parti- cular, my Andrew ? " and Jack waved his hands in the air. "Listen to me, good people,' he said. "You, most cherished brother, are going to efface yourself for one hour—one whole hour, mind you— and leave me to educate Midget, who is not at all all that she should be! Midget, my precious, you are going to sit down in that chair and be a good girl." . And Jack pushed her gently into the seat. "Well, I'm not jealous, and least of all of you, Jack! " said Andrew, preparing to go. Margaret looked curiously 14 Andrew's honest round face, and then turned with eyes a little brighter to the thin strong face of his brother. She noticed that his eyes seemed to have caught some of the brightness of her own. "And are you really going to leave me here to be bored for a whole hour. with .Jack? " she asked. "Of course! Why shouldn't I? He won't bore you; Jack's all right. He's quite mad, but he's ail right, and I want you to be friends. I'll go and join mother at Mrs. Brent's." "Smuggins," murmured Jack. "Well, I'm off " said Andrew. "Don't go," said Margaret, with the very least litge bit in the world of nervousness in her voice. "Are you afraid of me ? " demanded Jack, "taring at her. "No," she said, returning his stare boldly. "-Very well, then; Andrew, you may go." And Andrew went over to Mrs. Brent's. "Why did you want to talk to me for an hour 9 " Margaret asked. "Because I wanted to find out all about you, Midget, my dear, and I couldn't with our good Andrew there."

Margaret laughed. "Do you know why I stayed ? " she asked. "Because you wanted to find out all about me, I suppose," said Jack. Margaret shook her head maliciously. "Not at all. I stayed because I want you to tell me what the name is you have discovered for Andrew." "I had forgotten Andrew! " said Jack reproach- fully. "Why couldn't you have done as much for this one hour ? " "Oh, don't be silly!" said Margaret impatiently. "Do tell me the name ? " A smile came into Jack's eyes and played with his mouth. "I had thought," he said carelessly, "of Buttonhead.' How does it appeal to you ? " "Buttonhead ? Oh, how lovely!" laughed Margaret delightedly, and then added remorsefully: "Oh, poor Andrew! How wicked of you, Jack!"

"You wouldn't think it was only a week since we first met," Jack said softly, and his eyes wandered round the room. "No, indeed," said Margaret. Her face lookcd red in the firelight, and she screened her eyes with her hand. "The scene is just the same," Jack went on. "Same room, same hideous ornaments, same fire, same teapot, same absence of Buttonhead." "Don't call him Buttonhead," said Margaret. "But that's his name in the play," Jack per- sisted; "this play—our play—where the scene is just the same, but where to-night the players are different." _ _ _

"How are they different? said the girl, with something like a start. "Yes," said Jack. "We have, or we shall have, two new players to-night." He got up, and then sat down again opposite the girl, where he could see her face. "Now for the birth of two beings, who will live for an hour and then die together in. the flame there," and he held his hand out to the fire.

"Explain," said Margaret. "They will come up out of our eyes, so watch. Can you see my eyes?" "Perfectly, thinks." "No—you are not near enough; come closer." Margaret laughed, and brought her chair right up,to Jack's, and they 'sat with their knees ahriost touching. "Come, now; that's better!" said he. "Now look at me, and tell me, first of all, are you in love with Andrew?" Margaret hesitated a little. "Ye-es—l suppose so," she said. "Lie the first! " counted Jack on his finger. Margaret reddened a little. "I think I am indignant!" she said. The man stared steadily a her. "Oh, no, you're not," he said. "And secondly, were you ever in love with Andrew?" "Of course! -Don't be stupid, Jack I" "Lie the second!" said Jack calmly. "Oh!" protested Margaret. "Quite so. Oh!" continued Jack,•his „eyes still fixed on her face. "Did you, or did you not, pal in with Andrew because you couldn't find, anybody better in Middlegate ?" "You're a harrid brute! And what a vulgar phrase-:-.' pal in' ! " said Margaret,gevading the question. "You are not asked to make any comments on ray phrases or on me, but to answer my questiou. And don't fidffet about on yout chair." • "I'm not. fidgeting; and I shall not answer that question!" said Margaret defiantly. "I am answered," he said. "And sow, don't answer in a hurry: just think it over. But if—if, mind you—you had had the good fortune to meet me first—don't interrupt, please ; it's an awfully bad habit—would you not have fallen in love with me?" Margaret lowered her eyes, and gave the ghost of a laugh. "You are an idiot! " she said. "Quite so; who denied it ? " said Jack. "That .is not the question. What we want to get at—and we beg you to observe that it is all being done in the most orderly way—is: Wouldn't you have loved me if you had met me first?" "No, madman!" said Margaret, fkir.reting nith the lace at her wrists. "Why should I " "You are equivccating my Midget," said Jack, I:.An;rig• f,rrard. "Would you not?"

" No ! " "Do try and speak the truth! " "No!" Jack laid his hand gently on her knee. "Would you not have loved me? " he said quietly. "No, no, no! " said Margaret, nearly crying. "Do leave me alone! " "You are getting hysterical! A bad sign," saia Jael. "Look at me, Midget." Margaret studied the pattern of the carpet with unusual interest. "Look at Me, Midget," he repeated in the gentlest of voices. And Margaret's blue eyes met his grey ones. "Wouldn't you have loved me.? he whispered.

Margaret's brain seemed to be swimming round. "It isn't fair; you're mesmerising me! " "Wouldn't you have loved me? " he whispered I again. The girl's face was pale, and they were both silent for a second. Jack still leant forward with his hand on her knee and his eyes fixed on hers. It seemed to both of them that thdir hearts were running a furious race with the little clock on the mantelpiece. The glow from the fire heightened the glow in their eyes, and flickered alternate red and white on to their faces. "Wouldn't you have loved me? " She saw the question on his lips, though it was spoken too softly for her to hear. "What's the good of struggling against him? " she thought; "he's too strong for me." "Well? " he asked. "Have it your own way ! " said Margaret, lean- ing back in her chair. "Hurrah I " laughed Jack softly. "Hurrah! hurrah! hurrah! My little Midget! And jump- ing up, he seized her by the waist off her chair, and swung round the room with her in a mad sort of dance. "Good heavens! " panted Margaret, when he had stopped. "How strong you are! Why, you lifted me off that chair as if I were a baby "That's exactly what you are, my little Midget --a baby pure and simple!" "I admit the former qualification, and use my own reservations as regards the latter," laughed Margaret, seating herself in Jack's chair. Jack laughed, and stood by the fire, resting his elbow on the mantelpiece. They looked at each other and smiled. "You seem to be in good spirits!" said Margaret. "Who wouldn't—with you? But let us be serious again for five minutes." "No. We're so jolly as we are. Don't be

serious again." "0 Midget, my little fairy ! " said Jack earnestly, "don't you wish that. I could take you away to some enchanted island, where we could live as king and queen, and hold a fairy Court? " "It sounds delightful! " "You would be the sweetest lady, in the world, and I would be your humblest slave. " "It certainly would have its charms. Unfortu- nately, you see, there are no enchanted islands, and you will not take me away! " "No," said Jack solemnly. "I must not take you away. You belong to Andrew." "I never did care a button for Andrew! " burst out Margaret. "A buttonhead ! " suggested Jack. "No, my dear; but you are his promised wife, and I do not take away with me the promised wife of my brother .—do i, my lady ? " "I wouldn't comet" said Margaret. "You wi I not be asked. But it will bb Oteaaiant to know that—oh, well, never mind. You are going to marry Andrew." "And see you here every day." "I," said Jack lightly, -"leave here to-night." Margaret jumped up from her chair with red cheeks. • _

"Yon are going away ? " "Yes." "Why?" "Because my sister-in-law would be a little too charming for my peace of mind." "You are going away because you love me?" asked Margaret, with her hand on Jack's arm.

• "You hve said it." "Oh, but," said Margaret, her cheeks a little paler and her eyes a little brighter, "don't go away. Jack ! " _ _

"Why should I stay ? " "Because—because—Jack, I love you too I" "Another reason why I must leave here at once. Besides, you don't really love me yet, my Midget; you're only fascinated and interested. You would certainly love me if I stayed much longer." "You have a charming idea of yourself!" said Margaret, with a little laugh. "Not more charming than the idea you have ef

"Well, I never! " "A very dull remark," said Jack calmly. "Could you ever," said Margaret, "be senious about anything ? " "I was never more serious in Any life! Strely you must see that! I have been a.,wav for three years. I came home on Monday, and I run away again on Saturday! I came, as I thought, to stay. It seems that I came only to go. However, it was worth the coming, and it's worth the going." "What is? " "To have seen you, my Midget," said Jack tenderly. "You think that I am going to stay hers and marry Andrew ? " "Yes, dear; you must," Margaret shook her head in dissent. "He'll be bete soon," she said glancing at the clock. "Jack—" And she hesitated a little. "Well?" Margaret sidled up to him. "Give me one kiss I before you go!" Jack put his arm round her, and drew her close • to his side. Margaret looked at him, her eyes shining. "You'll be married when I see you again," said Jack in a whisper. Margaret shook her head vigorously. "Yes, yes, yes!" said Jack. "And no, no, no! " persisted Margaret, looking - up at him invitingly; 'Do kiss me! I love you! " "You're not shy, commented Jack. The girl drew herself away from him indignantly, her cheeks flaming a fine red. "Yon brute!" she cried, and then sat down on the sofa sobbing. "Why did you ever come here!" she said presently. Jack was leaning his head on his hand. "I am going away to-night," he replied. "Don't be unkind to me, sweet lady. You do not know how unkina I am being to myself! " "You insulted me!" said Margaret, her eyes , firll of tears. "No, dear child. I could not insult you. But I am trying not to kiss you—not to kiss you!" he burst out again. "Good God! I'm praying all the gods in heaven to keep me from taking you in .rny arms and crushing all the very life out of you with kisses! " And he raised his hot face from his hands and walked violently up and down the room. Margaret looked a little scared at his vehemence, and in her heart loved him a good deal more for it. "Poor Jack 1 " she said softly. Jack stopped in the middle of the room. "And poor Margaret!" she added lightly. "It's a little bit of a struggle for her too, you know, Jack! Jack gave a movement forward, as if he. world have flung himself at her feet, then turned suddenly and walked over to the mantelpieze. There he stood leaning his bead on his elbows, and Margaret could hear him whispering Ao himself: "Midget! Midget! My little Midget!" "What shall I do?" she thought. "Andrew may come in at any moment; he mustn't see us like this! Jack," she said softly. "Well, girlie ? " said Jack, raising his head with an effort. "Think of Andrew! He'll be here presently. What are you going to say ? " "J must get cool," said Jack, . walking over to the window. "I shall say it is unfortunate that I am of such a roving disposition that I never can stay for any length of time in any particular place, and another day of Middlegate will drive me raving mad." "Will they accept that as the only explanation of your sudden departure ? " said Margaret. "They will accept what they get," said Jack. "They all think I'm mad, and I never was given to explanations. I must go and get my things together." "Where will you go to? " asked the girl. Jack went over to her and took both her hands in his. "I shall fly away to the land east of the sun, west of the moon,' " he said gently. I "Be serious ! " said Margaret impatiently.. "Oh. what does it matter, dear lass ?I' said Jack. "One place is the same as another. And wherever I go I shall carry you, with me, although I am leaving you behind. He took her in his arms, and let the girl rest against him. I"I mustn't kiss you," he said whisperingly; but he let his lips rest for a second on her hair. I "Good-bye, little Midget! Good-bye!" A footstep outside caused them to separate hurriedly, and Margaret went quickly over to the ooking-glass to see if she looked as if she had been crying, as Andrew mine in. Jack went up to his brother, and took hold of his arm. "I want to see you and mother upstairs," lie said. "What about? "asked Andrew. "You'll hear in a minute, Andrew, old boy," . aid Jack, pushing him gently through the door. "Go and find mother. You stay here, Midget," he said; "I shall only be five minutes," and he ollowed Andrew upstairs. Margaret, wandering forlornly round the room, rnuld hear their confused voices in the room show,

"lie's going away 1 Sll.l tr.ougnt dully. "I've only known him a week, and yet I feel as if he had filled up the whole of my life. I can't remember a time when he wasn't there. ome I lie's going. away I " and Margaret pressed her hand to her side, as if the thought gave her physical pain. . .

She seemed to have a-great thrObbing lump in her throat, and her eyes felt hot and tired. "It is so trrible," she thought, "that I have to sonceal what I feel! I mustn't cry before them; and 1 shan't be able to say good-bye or anything! 0 Jack, Jack, Jack 1 " As if in answer, the door opened, and Jack came In with his brother. His .mother was upstairs packing his few belongings. "Well, Midget,' said Jack gaily, "off again, you see! Not a minute to spare!" "I can't understand it at all," said Andrew solemnly. "Nobody rtsked you to," laughed Jack im- pudently. "But if you swear on your oath you'll never tell a s3ul, and least of all Midget there, ae ridadil.g eagrtte.' ' looked tell you the real reason that's driving me from M at him apprehensively, and Jack gave her a little reassuring smile. "I never take an oath about anything," said Andrew; "but, of course, I'll not mention it to anyone if you wish it." Jack laughed at his brother's solemnity, ana then leant forward and said to him in a loud whisper: "I can't stand Midget's shoes! " "Why, you're 'joking again! " said Andrew- reproachfully. it "It has just dawned upon you, my Andrew, hair ? "

"I thought you were serious." "You ought to know by now that lam never serious about anything—except my meals," he said as an after-thought. "The cab's there," said Margaret mournfully. "Oh, you can't be really going, Jack! Tell him,* she said, turning entreatingly to Andrew, "tit he mustn't go." "It's no good my arguing!" said Andrew. "When Jack makes up his mind about anything, wild horses won't make him alter it!" "Come and see me off," said Jack, going out to the front door.

His mother joined him on the step, and ventured tucother timid protest against this desertion of them. "And we thought you were going to be here for the wedding and all! " she said, tearfully. "Very sorry, mother; you'll have to get married without me!" he said, laughing at her long. face.

Margaret and Andrew came out on to the steps. "Well," said the latter, grasping his brother's hand, "if nothing will keep you here, good-bye, Jack, old fellow. Come back to us again when yot get tired of rushing about." "That will I!" said Jack heartily; and then ht turned to Midget. "Be good and happy, little Midget," he said, holding her hand tenderly. "Think of your brother sometimes. Be brave, too. The girl in the story must play her part well, you know. Good-bye! " "She shan't marry Buttonhead, all the same! • cried Midget after him, as he got into the cab. Jack smiled a little wistfully at her, and kissed his hand as the man drove off with him. "Buttonhead! FASHIONS OF TO-DAY,

During this month fashion is practically at a standstill, and there are few modes of any note so far as their novelty is concerned. The greater part of femininity at large is, however, deepry engrossed with the attractions presented by the sales and the countless bargains and reductions offered thereat, and until these are over; there is little else to be talked of or thought of. The January sales as we all know, come like a storm to eventually clear the atmosphere of the sartorial world for the advent of the Spring fashions. When yielding, however, to the allurements of a visit to these sales one must exercise a considerable amount of discretion in selecting articles, and restrain sternly any tendency to weakly yield to the temptation to purchase an article merely because it is cheap without first seriously considering whether we have, or are likely to have, any opportunity of wearing it. Very soiled finery should be altogether avoided, as, except in a few instances, it is seldom worth he purchasing. There are, however, one or

two articles of dress which may safely be invested in. Furs come first and foremost on the list of the prudent woman, as the mo-t fashionable styles can be secured at a really reasonable price, and will be found a comfor, on many of the cold days which are a feature of early winter in England, while they will be found to be quite in the height of the fashion next winter. _ _

The comfortable cloak shown in the first illut,tra,ion would be found a welcome addition to one's wardrobe, and the economical woman could have it made at home at a much lowei price than it could be purchased ready made. The cloak itself is formed of a pretty coloured cloth with a smooth surface, and is lin,(l throughout with a quilting of pale rose pink silk. The high collar and deep cape are bordered with fluffy brown fur, and are further ornamented by an applique of point lace. Of course, should one have to use the train or tramcar much en route to the theatre or dance, a deeper tone of cloth should be used. as the pale colour soils so easily and quickly. The coat sketched is quite one of the season's novelties, and can be warmly recommended to anyone. It is of the nature of a guard's coat, and altogether military and as mannish - looking as possible. For preference the material should be navy blue cloth with pipings of scarlet at the seams and edges, the lining of the coat being of the same warm colour. The back of the coat has the fulness there confined by a buttoned strap. A PRETTY HAT.

The price of these coats is greatly reduced during this dull season, but it is gener- ally better to have a garment of this kind made to measure, as otherwise the right amount of easy hanging fulness is not likely to be achievgd, and a too loose and shapeless garment, or one too tight and clinging to the figure, be the result. It requires good cutting to make a loose coat a success, and as a wrap of this nature will be found useful all the year round I should sincerely advocate going to a little extra expense to get the right thing. Millinery is much in the foreground of the wintc- sales, and one cannot do better than invest in a pretty hat such as is depicted should the 'opportunity offer. It is made of black straw, while the upturned brim is swathea with black pin-spotted tulle. From under the brim where a jewelled ornament is placed, a hand- some black ostrich feather curls round over the hair to the back in the manner so approved of at present. It is easy to pick up a straw shape of this kind and many of us are the possessors of a good ostrich feather, as fashion has smiled so approvingly on these as a trimming. so that all that is required is a few yards of black- spotted net to evolve a charming chapeau. Among the other things which I may advise my reader to purchase, are shirts and blouses of nearly every kind, only avoiding those of chiffon which has become soiled through handling. Silk skirts are also to be desired, and are really cheaper than at any other time of the year. Odd yards of lace and ribbon, or short lengths of piece lace can be used for so many purposes that they should also be secured whenever a real bargain in these is offered, ass should likewise trimmings of all sorts.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The attachment of an official residence to high offices has certain disadvantages, and Mrs. Temple must feel keenly her leave taking of Lambeth Palace. To Mrs. Davidson, the wife of the new Archbishop, it is like returning home, as she is the youngest daughter of Archbishop Tait, to whom Dr. Davidson became chap- lain and private secretary in 1877.. Dr. Davidson who is now in his 55th year has a thoughtful face, and fully looks his age. He is regarded as a moderately High Churchman, and like Dr. Maclagan, the Archbishop of York, he is a Scotsman, born and bred. The festivities at Dalmeny and Edinburgh, in celebra tion of the coming of age of Lord Dalmen-y, have attracted a good deal of attention, not only in Scotland but in Buckinghamshire and Surrey. The occasion has served to emphasise the fact that Lord Rosebery is ex- tremely popular with his tenantry, who have manifested their interest in the celebrations in a very marked manner. Lord Rosebery, on the other hand has taken care that his tenants and servants shall not be left out of the festivities, and as the owner of the Durdans, his beautiful seat in Surrey, has once more extended his hospitality to the inmates of Epsom Workhouse, who have been provided with a substantial dinner and other entertainments. The London tradespeople are full of hope that the coming season will be as brilliant and successful ks it was last year disappointing. There will be some new hhostesses and many charming diSbutantes,so it is thought I that entertaining will be more general than was the case last year, when the thoughts of Society folks were centred upon the Coronation. One of the beauties of the season will be Miss Mary Manners, the eldest I daughter of Lord and Lady Manners, and there will be at least one great heiress in Lady Mary Douglas Hamil- ton. She is the only child of the late Duke of Hamilton, and is consequently a granddaughter of the Duchess of Devonshire, who will, no doubt, give some great entertainment in her honour. The many one-time superstitions in connection with weddings, appear to be entirely overlooked nowadays. For example green, in the olden days, was an impossible colour for such occasions, but at several of the smart Society weddings which have taken place lately, green in divers shades has been much worn. Then again black used to be considered in the very worst of taste. Women, in mourning, discarded it for a wedding, or did not appear. We have changed all that. At the marriage of Miss Stanley, and that of Lady Muriel Stuart Erskine, quite a sprinkling of the guests were in black unrelieved. At another recent Society wedding, the bridegroom presented each "maid "with a heart locket, set with opals, and towards the end of last year there was a bride brave enough to appearat the altar, wearing these ill-omened gems. Superstition is now, happily "out of date." The cockade has disappeared from the millinery world, but an even less artistic form of trimming is the cloth, or ribbon" penwiper " finished off appropriately with a jewelled button in the centre, which is the whim of the moment. As regards shapes, in headgear, one of the newest,closely resembles the shovel hat of the foreign priest. This in velvet, with one long ostrich plume sweeping round from the front to fall well over the hair behind,and is being much patronised by brides- maids. The turban toque, and the Shah of Persia turban are too popular to be modish, and exclusive dressers will no longer don them. _ The pouched blouse has a formidable rival in the crossover bodice which for some months past has been creeping into favour. With the crossover pelerine we are all familiar, but in both ideas there is one point that should be borne in mind, and that is, to keep the cross- ing low down to the waist line, and so give an appear- ' ance of length to the figure. Another much approved style for evening bodices is that, showing the deep bertha, and high waist belt. Cut somewhat low, in the style of the old Court bodice, the berthe is headed with a 'tucker" threaded through with narrow baby ribbon or velvet. So smart, and dressy looking are many of the flannel blouses and SHIRTS, that they are quite possible for afternoon wear with SHIRTS to match. These blouses and SHIRTS are delightfully warm and comfortable, and the home worker will find them easy to build. Viyella is the fabric to be selected. The colours in which it is possible to get this material are exquisite, and there is no question of shrinking. Properly washed, a Viyella shirt or blouse can visit the tub again and again, and be wearable to the end of the chapter. By proper washing is metuat;care that the water is warm only; that the soap is placed in the water and lathered with the hands, not rubbed upon the blouse; that the garment is afterwards well rinsed in lukewarm water; wrung in a cloth; and immediately hung out of doors to dry. Short remnants of Oriental embroideries, to be had very cheaply at the sales, will come inuseful for trimming these particular garments, but tley should be used sparingly. Some of the most successful morning shirts, and blouses, are the most siMple, which boast nothing in the way of ornamenta- tion save stitchery. The old-fashioned counterpane is never seen now-a days, and one cannot regret its disappearance. It was heavy, as regards weight, and very =artistic. The newest "bed-spreads," as they are called, are in pure white linen, embellished, more or less lavishly with designs in drawn thread-work. Under these designs a colour is sometimes laid, the same tint:appearing under table mate, pillow-slips, and night-dress case, which all match. There are many housewives however, who pre- fer an all-white scheme, it is so much more dainty they say. This is simply a matter of taste, but care of course is necessary in the selection of the colours chosen. • They must be of the palest and most delicate, and blend with or match the walls, carpet, and hangings. Several of Gainsborough's pictures have had a remark- able history, and a few years ago a tattered canvass, which had been lying by for years, was sold by auction for £6. The purchaser having discovered that it was a painting by Gainsborough of Miss Ethel Duncombe, a famous beauty in her day, sold it for £lB3 to a print- seller in London. He before long agreed to sell it to the late Lord Chesterfield for a thousand guineas, but owing to the death of the noble lord, the transfer was not completed, and the vendor having consented to cancel the sale, sold the picture to a member of the Rothschild family for fifteen hundred guineas. i With "The visits of Elizabeth" we were all delighted land with a later work -Recollections of Ambrosine,"by I the same author (Elinor Glyn) we shall be equally charmed. "Ambrosine" a brilliant, lovable little creature, tells the story herself, and tells it with ease, and skilful grace. Messrs Duckworth have published both these delightful books

Here is her own story : "Up to July, 1901, I hardly ever had a day's sickness but in that month I became unwell owing to getting wet. The ailment settled on my liver, so the doctor said, and for three weeks I was in bed. I trembled Violently all over from liver chills, and bad acute head- aches. I was subject to violent retching, and vomited up everything I took. I was much affected with giddi- ness and often fainted away. The doctor was in attend- ance upon me for nearly three months, but long after he ceased to attend me I was subject to fainting tits. The fact was, I never got properly well:

"It seems to me, said An(lrew Bates, carefully selecting the thinnest piece of toast, "that before you give your opinion of Margaret you might mait and see her."

EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

THE HANDSOME NEW HEAD OFJ

THE NEW POST OFFICE SAVINGS SANK.

The Post Office Savings Bank is about to remove from its old offices in Queen Victoria Street, 'London, to a splendid new erection in Kensington. Some idea of the magnificence of the exterior of this building may be gathered from our illustra- tion, and the interior is equally grand. The purchase gf the site was effected with a cleverness not often forthcoming from a Government Department. Instead of asking the price of the vendors direct, one of the chief officials wrote from his private address asking the lowest quotation, and indicating that he was prepared to buy the site for building purposes if the price were reasonable. The vendors, without the ghost of a suspicion as to whom they were dealing with, promptly replied that to a bona fide- quick pur- chaser the pried would be £50,000. The official at .once accepted their offer, went to Somerset House to get the letter stamped, and then transferred the purchase to the postal authorities. One of the new features at Ic.-:nsington is the installation of a large prinfin.: plant. which is to be worked by electricity, and by means of which in future the Savings Bank Department will do all its own printing.

IFFICZ SAVINGS BANK.

DISAPPEARING DICKENS'S LONDON.

One by one, the innumerable places in London immortalised by Dickens are being swept away at the ruthless hands of the modern architect. Many of these spots, now merely sites swept clean of any stick or stone which might even suggest what once stood there, were looked upon by the great man, and selected for the locality of some character or scene which has be. come part and parcel of English literature Bo long as the English language will last. One of the most recent disappearances has been the King-- gate Street which was known of Sairey Gamp. The Southampton Row improvements have made a

ET IS NOW DEMOLISHED FOR THE SOUTHAMPTON ROW WIDENING. _

clean sweep of it, and left not a wrack behind. All lovers of Dickens will be glad, therefore, to have a sketch of the street while it yet stood much as it was when Poll Sweedlepipe's barber's shop was there. It is the econd house on the right in the drawing, "next door but one to the celebrated mutton pie shop," where Sairey lodged "in the first floor front." But though we think of Kingsgate Street only in connection with the characters which the magic of the novelist has raised for us, Kingsgate Street was in its time a historic locality. The ex- cellent Pepys, whose quaint gossip leads us far and wide over London, has recorded for us in his Diary :—" To Whitehall, from whence the King (Charles II.) and the Duke of York went by three in the morning, and had the misfortune to be over- set, with the Duke of York, the Duke of Monmouth, and the Prince (Rupert), at the King's Gate in Holborne ; and the King all dirt but no hurt. How it came to pass I know not, but only it was dark, and the torches did not, they say, light the coach as they should." Kingsgate Street, it seems, was so called because the King used to go by that way on his way out of London to Newmarket.

NEW DEPUTY MASTER OF THE MINT.

The appointment of that popular Orangeman, the Rt. Hon. W. G. Ellison Macartney, to be Deputy-Master of the Mint has given very general satisfaction in lobby circles. The appoint- ment is in succession to the late Sir Horace Seymour, and in consequence a vacancy will be created in the representation of South Antrim. Mr. Macartney has represented that constituency in the Unionist interest since 1885, and his majority at the general election over Mr. S. Lowther, also a Unionist, was 593. He is a fluent speaker and an energetic worker. 'Mr. Macartney acted as Parliamentary Secretary to the Admiralty from 1895 to 1900, when, after the General Election, he was made a member of the Privy

He was one of the 'Unionist minority who marched out of the Committee on the Irish Land Acts when their minority report was rejected. He is a director of the London and North Western Railway, and takes a keen interest in all things appertaining to the line.

THE NEW ARCHBISHOP.

The new Archbishop of Canterbury, the Rt. Rev. Randall Thomas Davidson, is a man who was, so to speak, born with a silver spoon in his mouth, and from his earliest days in the Church he has lived under the shadow of the Primacy. Born in 1848, the great year of revolution, he WJS educ,atetl at Harrow and Oxford, and was ordained in I 7 by the Bishop of Dover. Becoming a curate at Dartford, he ob- tained his first and only paro- chial experience • in that district, though there are probably few, if any, clergymen who have seen less:actual active service in a par- ish, none have known the minor workings of the Church itself for a longer period. He was now made Dean of Wine,or. ants becalw a great favourite at Court. In l:•;:o n.• succeeded Dr. Thorold as Bishop of Roci,ester, and, having refused the See of Bath and toui, be place of the same prelate, upon his death in 196, at Winch,ster. THE NEW WESTERN AUSTRALIAN GOVERNOR.

The governorship of Western Australia has been. vacant for some little time owing to the appointment of Sir Arthur Lawley to the post of Lord Miler's second in command in the Trans- vaal after a short but successful period of administration. Now it is officially announced that he will be succeeded by Admiral Sir Frederick George Denham Bedford, G.C.8., who has had a long and distinguished career in the Navy. He first saw service in the Crimea as a midshipman on H.M.S. Sampou, receiving th e Crimean, Turk- ish, and Baltic medals, ad the Sebastopol clasp. In 1877 he com- manded 1-1.31.5. THE EVICTION OF DENABY.

Probably unequalled in the troubled annals af trades unionism are the recent scenes at Denaby, in Yorkshire, where the protracted struggle of the miners on strike has led to the wholesale eviction of themselves and their families from their homes, and which is still causing them acute suffering. The evictions were carried out by the police at the order of the mine owners, and it is to be recorded to the credit of the strikers that not one instance of resistance has been met with, the men, women, and children removing their belongings, and departing in a most orderly manner. This fact has not lowered them in the estimation of the public, nor been the means of a reduction in the sympathy of

SCENES OF THE PASSIVE EXODUS OF TUE DENADY EASIILIEs.

01 those who have, throughout the struggle looked v ill admiration on their determination .o fight to the bitter end. Altogether, about 7-10 families have had to leave in consequenw of ejectment ors. There are streets and streets where hat dly a single window blind can be seen in a long row of brick hon-es. The place is half empty, and a population enough to form a large village ha .4 been timed away. Several neighbouring chapels are full of people, and many others of those evicted have found refuge with friends. At the close of the evictions the police thanked the strike com- mittee for the assistance and good sense shown in deprecating disorder. Most of the . police left Denaby on Friday, only 40 remaining out of ever 200.

SLAVES OF THE SHOP.

Parliament May Help Them.

THE organizers of the Shop Assistants' Union are looking anxiously for the day when Parliament will spare time from mismanaging the War Office to consider their special grievances. That day may be nearer than the most hopeful imagine. The country depends upon the vigour of the rising generation, for we are faced by foreign competition, and can scarcely look for virility and vigour among thosewho are forced to followa close and trying occupation—as Shop Assistants are—twelve to fifteen hours a day. Nor are these the only sufferers, writes a representa- tive of the Lincolnshire Echo ; and he proceeds to give a story told by Mrs. Lily Cooke? of 1, Bedford-street, Lincoln, who was cured of mamma and its resultant disorders, by Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, after suffering for many years. "I used to suffer dreadfully," said Mrs. Cooke. "I took iron and quinine and various patent medicines, yet continued to be as weak as a kitten. I was pale and languid, and always felt tired. I couldn't walk upstairs without resting, and worst of all was the terrible back- ache that never left me. I could not sleep, and what I ate gave me no benefit. I used really to think that I was going to die. Now I eat anything I fancy, and with a good appetite, thanks to Dr. Williams' pink pills. Since I took them, all thedreadful symptoms of anminia have disappeared—backache (that terrible backache!), weakness, depression, and all. "Although I was very ill when I began the first box, I was decidedly better before it was finished. I kept on taking the pills, and soon I was entirely changed from a tired and weary woman with very little interest in life to the healthy person you see me now. A friend of mine had eczema in her hands, face, and headl and was in a dreadful state. I know that Dr. Williams' pink pills cured her. There was a woman in Doncaster about my age,. who suffered terribly from backache. The pills cured her too. She said "I should not be alive but for them.'"

"Too little blood" is the cause of so much disease and men as well as women suffer from this cause. When any part of the system lacks "tone," or strength, Dr. Williams' pink pills have an extraordinary faculty of , restoring it. The price is two shillings and ninepence, neat direct by the manufacturers, Dr. 'Williams' medi- i eine company, Holborn-viaduct, London, or obtainable iof medicine dealers. Intending purchasers are some- times deceived into accepting substitutes, but they will find safety by insisting on seeing the name in full, Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, on the packet re. ceived. An article on the cure of rheumatism by these pills will be found on another page. They are especi- a!ly good for the weakness and misery which indoor 4 life, the "Living-in System," and bad air entail upon I Shop Assistants.

Dareshury Petty Sessions.

TUESDAY.—Before Messrs. J. C. Parr (presiding). W Long, H. Neild and Joseph Knowles. UNHAPPY MARRIED LlFE.—William Henry Wilkin- son, of Stretton was charged with persistentcruelty to- wards his wife, Sarah Ann, whereby she had been com- pelled to leave him.—Mr. Browne appeared for the com- plainant and Mr. Steel for the defendant.—Mr. Browne said the parties were married on the 25th May, 1901, at St. Thomas's Church, Stockton Heath, and they had one child. Defendant had ill-used his wife ever since the marriage, but he (Mr. Browne) would not go back further than the 2nd of April, 1902, when defendant locked his wife up all night because she would not give him money for drink. He went on the "spree "for a week or a fortnight at a time. Five weeks ago he commenced to go on the "spree" and had not done a stroke of work since. The wife had been dependent on the neighbours and her parents for food. He had struck her on the head and arms and had used filthy language to her, and events culminated on Saturday night, January 3rd, when he came home at 7 o'clock very strongly under the influence of drink. She asked him where he had been and he replied "Shut your mouth." Mr,. Browne detailed the conversation which ensued, and described the defendant's visit to two public- houses in the village of Stretton to which places his wife followed him remarking that "if he could afford to stay in public-houses she could do the same." When they were in the lane on their way home the defendent struck his wife four times on the head and neck and knocked her down, dazing her. Some neighbours picked her up and carried her into the house. Here he threw her across the kitchen and struck her again several times and told her to "clear out." This was said in the piesence of the mother. On the following morning, on the advice of her &other, complainant left the house. The defendant asked her to return, promising "to be a better man," but she had refused to do gp and now asked for a separation order and for alimony for herself and child. The defendant, Mr. Browne said, was "simply a drunken brute who would not work and maintain his family."—Evidence having been given, Mr. Steel, for the defence, submitted that this was a question of bad temper on the part of the complainant and her mother; if it had not,been for that bad temper the Bench would not have been troubled with the case. The complainant's story was very much exaggerated. The evidence disagreed on very important particulars, and he contended that if the Bench could not believe the witnesses in one thing they could not in another. He ventured to submit that the Bench were, therefore, not entitled to believe them. The defendant was a hard-working man. He occasionally got drunk, but it was owing to his wife's conduct. Mr. Steel called nei- ther the defendant nor any witness.—The Bench decided to grant a separation order ;and ordered defendant to pay defendant 6s. a week towards the mainten- ance of his wife, who will have the custody of the child.

Before (Mr. Long (in the chair), Mr. Neild and Mr Knowles.

ASSAULT.—Henry Carter, a young man employed at a chemical works at Winnington, was summoned for an assault on Sarah Jane Hayes, a young woman, at Dutton, on the 10th inst.,-Mr. Browne appeared for the complainant.—A fine of 20s andcosts,lor,in default, a • month's imprisonment was inflicted.

Manchester and Liverpool District Banking Co.

The shareholders of this company met on Wednes- day in Manchester, Mr. Abel Buckley presiding. The directors recommended a dividend for the half-year of 15s. a share and a bonus of ss. a share, both free from income tax. It was reported that with a view to the adjustment of the relation of capital to reserve, the directors considered it advisable that a portion of the reserve fund should be capitalised, and to carry out this object they recommended the declaration of a especial bonus of .1:2 per share to be paid thereout, and on this bonus being declared a call of a like amount should be made, which, when due, share- holders would be enabled to meet by means of the special bonus. The shareholders unanimously adopted these recommendations.

CUMBERLAND v. LANCASHIRE

Played at Alillinu. In the Lancashire team noardman of Warrington, displaced Frnter, of Oldham, and Lomas took the place of W. James at half back, whilst on Cumberland's side Robley. and Messenger, of Maryport, and T. Dixon, of Parton, filled the vacancies caused by Little, of Halifax, Lomas of Salford, and Westmore- land, of Whitehaven Recreation. The atmospheric con- ditions were wretched, and the attendance of spectators was considerably effected. Beetham started for Cumber- land against the wind, and from the first scrummage a combined movement by the Lancashire backs culminated in Hogg scoring a try, Lomas failing to improve. The re-start was again favourable to Lancashire, and another round of passing saw Wilson run ' over the line. S. James converted. Cumberland, how- ever, were not daunted by their opponents' success, and Ben Wilson gave his side a footing in Lancashire terri- tory. Lomas and Dickinson were tackled, and then from a forward burst,Timmoney followed up and scored, the goal kick failing. Play was even for a short period, but the speed of the visiting backs gave James a favour- able opportunity, and another concerted movement ended in Hogg scoring James adding the extra points. Half-time score:—Lancashire 2 goals and 3 tries (11 points), Cumberland 1 try (3 points). In the second half Cumberland had the breeze behind them, but they failed to take advantage of it. The for- wards were beaten in the packs, and Timmoney had to save. Kitchen kicked up the field, and from some loose play inside Lancashire "25" Fish gathered the ball,and with a brilliant effort he rounded the backs and scored a splendid try. The goal kick was unsuccessful. Lanca- shire continued to hold the upper hand, and a judicious pass from Wilson to Hogg saw the winb.man again run over. James improved. There was little more of in- terest, except when James dropped against the goal- posts after a tricky run, and when Beetham was ordered to the touch-line by the referee. The game ended in a win for Lancashire by 3 goals and 5 tries (21 points),to Cumberland 1 try (3 points). Teams:— Cumberland.—J. Robley (Maryport). back; B. Wil- son (Barrow). J. H. Dixon (Parton). J. Smallwood (`Whitehaven Recrea- tion), M. Vartv and E. K. Tinnion (Seaton), J. Bean (Maryport), S. Hoggarth(Millom), and H. Templeton Workington (forwards). Lancashire.—D. Smith (Salford), back; A. Hogg and R. Wilson (Broughton Rangers), G. Dickinson and J. Fish (Warrington), three-quarter backs; S. James (Broughton Rangers) and J. Lomas (Salford) half backs; P. Tinney, J. Rhapps, and G Heath (Salford , A. S. Boardman (Warrington), J. Ferguson (Oldham, W. Oram (Broughton Rangers), G. Boardman (Leigh, and J. Evans (Swinton), forwards. Referee, Mr. J. M. Sedman, Yorkshire; Touchjudges, Messrs. W. T. Bennett, Cumberland, and J. H. Hough- ton, Lancashire. HULL v. BROUGHTON RANGERS.

This match, which was played on the Hull Boulevard Ground, was shorn of much of its interest owing to the visitors having been thinned of their best men, half-a- dozen of them having to appear in the Lancashire V. Cumberland match. Hull pressed, and Frank and Thompson made a fine opening for Parry, who had only to cross the line. He succeeded, and Woodhead added the goal. After a sprint down the left wing Widdeson was so badly injured, in a tackle that he had to leave the field. Frank broke away splendidly from the next scrummage, and, although beset with opposition, scored. Woodhead failed to convert, but just before half time Jackson taking the ball from Jacques, got into position, and nearly dropped a goal, bringing the score to 10 points to nil.—An indifferast, start was made after the interval, but gradually Broughton improved their play, and took the contest into Hull ground. Their attack was excellent now and then, but flukey tactics spoiled the display. Hull missed a chance after a mark by Parry, but they were able to put on three more points with a try in the corner by Parry, which was unconver- ted, and the game ended :—Hull 2 goals 3 tries (13 points), Broughton nil. LEIGH v. HULL KINGSTON ROVERS.

Played at Leigh, before over 2,000 spectators. At the interval neither side had scored. Leigh had the slope with them in the second half, and after fine kicking by Johnson the Hull line was hotly besieged. Eventually Johnson smartly dropped a goal. Morfitt afterwards got away smartly, but Ganley was too fast for him, and a kick by Pearson, removed play again to the Rovers' end, where Smith got hold and made an attempt to score, but was knocked into touch. In the last few minutes B. Kirk made a strong dodgy run, but Sinclair brought him down near the line. Result—Leigh 1 goal (2 points), Hull Kingston Rovers nil.

THE LEACTUE.---FIRST DIVISION.

BRIGHOUSE RANGERS v. WARRINGTON.

Warrington took a weak team to Brighouse on Satur- day, County calls requiring Fish, Dickinson, and Board- man, while Isherwood was absent injured. The home side were well represented and managed to defeat War- rington by 7 points to nil. Wretched weather prevailed and there was only a small attendance. The teams were

.• _ _ Brighouse.—F. Saville, back; C. Baker, E."Sutcliffe W. Walker, and Lewis Brooke, three-quarter backs; d. Denham and F. Bevan, half backs • G. Kilburn, Sam Walton, F. Woffenden, E. Wilson, G. W. Sparke, J. B. Hodgson, F. Miller, and J. A. Holroyd forwards. Warrington.—Knight,back; Harris, Hallam, Edmunds, and Bailey, three-quarter backs; Hockenhall and Hunter, half backs; J. Swift, E. Swift; Morrison, Harmer, Edmundson, Naylor, Roberts, and Cunningham, for- wards. Referee, Mr. H. Kennedy; touch judges Messrs. G. W. Gibson (Keighley) and H. W. Depledge (Huddersfield.) Brighouse won the toss and selected to play down the slope. Denham, Bevafi, and Sutcliffe indulged in smart passing, and from a somewhat acute angle Saville landed a fine goal. Brooke put in a good kick, which was well returned, rand the Warrington men tried a few rounds of passing. Bailey got away, but stepped into touch. Saville kicked across, and Denham had a nice chance of a trv,but failed to take the opportunity. Saville almost landed a second goal from a penalty, after which,smart passing between Saville, Sutcliffe, and Baker ended in the latter scoring in the corner, and Saville landed a goal. At half-time Brighouse were leading by seven points to Warrington nil. Saville restarted, and the ball eventually went into touch at the centre. Saville put in a splendid kick up the slope, and Sutcliffe and Baker seemed to have chances which were not taken. Saville was pressed, but ran well, and transferred to Baker, who kicked in excel- lent style, and Knight was forced to kick over his own line, but by means of a penalty kick Warrington secured relief. Brooke seemed to be over at the corner, but was brought down on the line. Nothing was scored in this half, and Brighouse won by 2 goals 1 try (7 points) to Warrington nil. SALFORD v. WIGAN.

This match took place at the Willows, Weaste, be- fore 4,000 people. Brown started for Salford, who were first to attack. Subsequently Rouse broke away for the visitors, and despite the attentions of Preston and Shore scored in a favourable position Bothwell landed a goal. Half-time score—Wigan 1 goal 1 try (5 points), Salford nil. On resuming,Sallord made several good attempts to break through the Wigan defence, Bell being conspicuous. The visitors cleared well to the centre, and Dickinson in a scramble, scored a try, the

THE UNIVERSE.

Warrington, January) 1903.

How wonderful, how grand, The Universe appears, How infinite sublime, On the eternal year s. Beyond all human thought Or fancies highest flight, Whence came the orb of day, And all the stars of nighl. Was there in eons past, Before their wondrous light, A state of nature wrapt, In dark chaotic night. Unthinkable a first, Whatever thought we spend, We find no beginning, We cannot see an end.

From the unbeginning, • Eternal energy, Change in the Universe, Goes on eternally. Evalying all that is Pain, pleasure, happiness. That which is not needed, Becoming ever less. Has all forever been, - When changing atoms give .The consciousness of life, Does it forever live. From low forms advancing, Though death by slow degree, Each life something gaining, Some choice remaining free. *Do justice wisdom love Beyond all changes last, And make for kindred souls A. heavenly world at last.

S. KENYON.

STOCKTON HEATH.

FOWL STEA LER SENT TO GOAL.—At the Warrington Police Court, on Monday, before Mr. Monks and other magistrates, James Higham, 8, Garnett's-yard, was charged with being in possession of two dead fowls in Mersey-street, on Saturday night, the same being sup- posed to have been stolen.—Superintendent Monaghan stated that P.C. Sankey saw the man in Mersey-street with the fowls in his possession at about half-past. seven. He was unable to account for them satisfactorily, and it had since been ascertained that they had been stolen from Mr. Vere B. Davies, London Bridge, Stockton Heath. As the house was not in the Warring- ton County Borough Police district he asked that. prisoner be handed over to the Cheshire Police.— This was agreed to, and the prisoner was subsequently brought up at the Warrington Occasional Court and remanded to Daresburn Petty Sessions.—The prisone- appeared at the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, when he pleaded guilty to the charge, and was 'sent to goal for two months with hard labour.—The Chairman characterized it as "an unblushing robbery."--Continu- ing he said the Bench were glad to see the Warrington police acting in conjunction with the County police. They would always be glad to reciprocate, and they were very pleased with the.action of Police-Constable Sankey, who apprehended the prisoner in Warrington.— Superintendent Ennion : It was a smart capture of Constable Sankey.. . . .

The Hull trawler Othello. which arrived home on Wednesday, reported. that while fishing off Tceland, the third hand, named Birch, became entangled in the gear- ing of the winch, which separated his head from the body.

Harry Knowles, a police constable, was on Wednes- day tined ss. and costs by the Birmingham magistrates for assaulting a youth whom he was arresting. Mr. Arthur Chamberlain, in announcing the magistrates' de- cision,said they did not believe the police evidenoein the case. They did not think the police witnesses had done their best to put the Bench in possession of th facts. Ft,„!,,A IU

THE public is nottiow THE LONDON AND likely to be baulked GLOBE AFFAIR, of such satisfaction as may be obtained by a legal investigation of the affairs of the London and Globe Finance Corporation. A perfect storm of disapproval has been aroused by the refusal of the ATTORNEY-GENERAL to authorise a prosecu- tion by the Crown. The circumstances are such that there is a very extensive insinuation of un- worthy motives. Great names are familiarly associated with what has come to be called the London and Globe scandal; but a 3 it has become a common practice to link great names, not much importance needs to be attached to this circum- stance. The ATTORNEY-GENERAL'S position is ex- plicable without supposing him to be corrupt. At the sanle time, it is one that cannot be expected to meet with approval, for it; is based on the narrow principle that the Crown is not called upon to interfere in any case of this nature un- less its legal advisers have satisfied themselves that a Jonviction will certainly be secured. This is to shut the door with a clang in the face not only of the London and Globe shareholders, but practically of all those who have been losars in a similar way, and by a like method of finance. To satisfy himself that there must certainly be a con- viction, the ATTORNEY-GENERAL must try the case beforehand, which is ogviously impossible. His position is, surely, much the same as that of a grand jury. What he has to decide is blot the question of guilty or not guilty, but whether there is enough evidence to go before a court. To take a severer view than thiA of his responsibilities in such a case is to constitute himself an obstacle to judicial investigations that may be necessary in the.. public interest.

Those who have followed the London and Globe revelations must have asked themselves with some surprise what evidence the Attorney-General would require before permitting a Crown prosecu- tion if the evidence in this Case is deemed to be insufficient. No doubt many things are said that it might not be possible to prove. It is not in- conceivable that the heads of the Loudon and Globe Corporation may have been astute enough to iep the letter of the law on their side, but it islet least highly unlikely ; and if they have been so ingenious as to do this,it may well be doubted whether there are any circumstances under which the law is really able to protect the investor. For That is it that appears to have taken place P It is alleged, on grounds certainly worthy of investigation, and, indeed, quite as certainly dPrnanding it, that the Lon- don and Globe Corporation maintained its credit by a process of shifting its liabilities from one account to another. New ventures were

===== 0002601_19030116_art0060.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
NIMPI

===== 0002601_19030116_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
called in being for the express purpose, it would seem, of making it possible to successfully misre- present the position of companies already in •existence and beyon I help by any less exception- able means. An apparent stock of cash in hand was obtained by a sale of shares which were to b 'bought back, immediately the balance-sheet had 'been passed. Lord DUFZERIN and Lord Loon, men of high mind and character, were so trustful, ,or little careful of their responsibilities as mem- bers of the bard, that they signed a statement to the effect that as the Corporation was in a position to pay a 'regular dividend of 10 per cent. anything over this would be given as a bonus. The shares would then, said the directors,"be taken out of the speculative category and soon be classed as an investment security,from which the proprietors can count on receiving a specific return .with an unfailing regularity." This statement was sent out with the balance-sheet. It had, of course, no foundation in fact. If 10 per .cent were to be obtained with unfailing regularity we should all hasten to invest in a concern offering such golden opportunities. It may be the op;nio n of the Attorney-General that things of this kind can be done without an actual breach of the law. That would be a strong case for further legisla- tion; but in the meantime the average man will refuse to believe that the law is so ineffective as Sir ROBERT FINLAY'S decision seems to suggest; and he will affirm that in any case:that inefiective- nessshould be demonstrated in court. if only to provethe necessity for a further addition to Mae statutes.

_ However, the London and Globe affair will now be brought into the courts without doubt. The promoters of the two actions, one against the directors of . the Corporation and one against Ma. WHITAKER WRIGHT individually, have coalescea, and a fund i 9 accumulating for the purpose of pro viding the necessary expenses of prosecution. When a sum of £5,000 has been raised an applica- tion will at once be made to the High Court, and the public will then be relieved of the pain of thinking that such proceedings as are charged in this instance may be freely indulged in without risk of legal investigation and punishment. That the necessary funds will be forthcoming there can be little doubt. Many of those who have suffered loss will say that it is useless to throw good money after bad. The ATTORNEY-GENERAL'S veto must be apt to produce that kind of depressed convic- tion. But there are, we imagine, more than enough determined men among the aggrieved shareholders to see this thing through.

THE practical arid admitted failure of the crusade against pirated music affords the most vivid illustration in modern times of the truth o' the saying about driving a coach and four through any Act of Parliament. If ever a measure looked certain to carry into effect the wishes of its pro- moters it was the Musical Copyright Act of last session. In fact, there were many by no means averse to the protection of a man's property in the product of his own brain who considered its pro- visions dangerously drastic. Yet a single decision of the High Court, and a better organization among the printers and vendors of pirated music —somewhat on the principle of those thieves who used to hang together lest they should hang separately—and the Act on which the music publishers placed such reliance, has become almost, if not quite a dead letter. The men who offer copyright pieces for twopence in the atreets of London, humble as they look, have their scouts on the watch, and if an agent of the Musical Publishers' Protection Society appears in the street the word is passed, and every unauthor- ised copy in the neighbourhood disappears as if bY magic. Then, to avoid the necessity of having any large stock on hand, the sellers have lately taken to circularising quiet suburban neighbour- hoods, and, in fact, calling for orders. Similarly, if a pirated issue is traced to its place of origin,the printer is now found provided with an agree- ment leasing part of his premises to some person who is represented as the real culprit, and who is, •of course, a man of straw, against whom, even if he can be found, it would be utterly idle to take proceedings. The whole business illustrates sadly the limitations of the effectiveness of Parlia- mentary action.

TEE British Commercial Agent at Mexico, ie. his report on the year's trade of the Republic, takes up the old parable. British traders might do very well in Mexico, though the United States of course, must be expected to take the largest share of business. Yet, however much the trade of the country increases, the imports from the 'United Kingdom decrease, for the reason, says the report, that British manufacturers and mer- chants in general will not adopt more modern Methods. For instance, in the competition With Germany it is found that though german articles are admittedly of inferior quality tb British, their style and finish are such „as to compare with goods of British origin cost- ing twice the amount. Again, it is stated that german makers are far more ready than English to accept any suggestions made to them,either by their own agents or by clients, who are often More in touch with the requirements of the country, and are eager to see new and improved articles placed upon the market, not only for their "11 benefit, but also for that of their fellow- countrymen. It is also suggested that English Merchants might profitably follow the example of the Americans in sending numerous agents to push ilst sale of their wares,especially as while a know- ledge of §panish is desirable ic is not as neces- sary as former 4, English having so far come into Use as to be understood by the greater number of those employed in commerce. British trade is further impeded, it ib suggested,by extreme care- lessness on the part of the home merchants in consigning goods or in declaring their character under the Mexican tariff, the result being often a delay of six 'months. We are getting used to this sort of lecture, perhaps,and it does not disturb our complacency as it should. Still some thanks ard due to our representatives abroad who tell us so candidly of oar faults.

• Compliments are so seldom paid to Warrington ,_om outside that it is a pleasure to find "The Nethodist Weekly" devoting a leading article to a review of the booklet on The Beginnings of Total Abstinence," recently quoted by us, and pay- ing enthusiastic tribute to Warrington's record as the birthplace of the Temperance Movement. ;This journal says :—"The famous seven men of Preston alre to be cast down from their pedestal. They aid not constitute the first Total Abstinence Society. A pamphlet by Mr. Arthur Mounfield, just issued, puts the disputed matter for ever at rest by documentary evidence. Henceforth the laurels must be taken from Preston, and placed Upon the brows of Warrington. There are three things to rejoice over, despite our sorrow for un- crowned Preston. First, the Methodists had a first hand in this great movement; second, that 'Quakers had the honour of origination ; and third, that Manchester gave the movement its 6xst great invetus, , . Warrington may hold up its head on high, and when England becomes teetotal, as God grant it may, it will hold its head up still higher."

RUNCORN AND WIDNES,.

REV. FATHER CHAMBERS.

The Rev. Father Chambers, of St. Edwards' Catholic Church, Runcorn, has been appointed Rector of St. Werburgh's, Chester, in succession to the late Rev. Canon Lynch. He will leave Runcorn at the end of the present month.

SUDDENT DEATH OF A WOMAN,

The death has been reported to the policc of Mrs Breadman, aged 48, of 43, Stanley-street, Runcorn. Her husband, who is an engine driver in the employ of the Manchester Ship Canal Company, went to bed at nine o'clock on Friday night leaving deceased and two chil- dren downstairs. Later in the night he was awakened by his wife coughing. She said, "Oh, hold my head up, lam choking." He jumped out of bed and held her head up. She died in his arms. Dr. Delaney was sum- moned to the house. He had been attending deceased for sometime for heart disease.

"Y" BRANCH .OF THE RUNCORN WOMEN'S TEMPER- ANCE ASSOCIATION.

The. monthly meeting of the above society was held on Wednesday in Ellesmere-street school which was kindly lent for the occasion. Mrs. Morris presided and said that it was not self-interest or ambition that made people devote their energy to this work, but love for fellow creatures. Miss Marple of Birkenhead, told how she became a worker again t the national curse of a Christian nation and urged the members to do likewise. Mrs. Anders and Miss Ryalls gave recitations, Mrs. V. Ellison sang, and the Misses Handley gave a pianoforte due'. The singing of the Temperance Doxology brought the meeting to a close.

CLAIM FOR COAL

Before his Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C., at the Runcorn County Court on Tuesday, the Wigan Coal and Iron Company claimed .e 4 from William Henry Charlesworth for coal. Mr. Brandreth agent for the plaintiff company said the defendant formerly lived in Runcorn. He purchased coal from them, and an account for 24 14s. 6d. was rendered to him. He had discharged the 14s. 6d.—Defendant said that in 1900 his brother bought the business from him, and purchased the coal from the plaintiff company. It was stated that defend- ant had been to South Africa with the Runcorn Volunteer Special Company.—Judgmcnt was given, for the plaintiffs.

THE OUTBREAK OF SM ALL-POY.

Two cases of small-pox have occurred in the Runcorn district. It will be remembered that the man Howarth, who was found to be suffering from the disease at St. Helens; had previously stayed at a lodging-house in Fryer-street, Runcorn. Every precaution was taken to prevent a further spread of the disease, but notwith- standing the prompt action of the authorities two further cases have occurred. The first is that of • a casual labourer who has been staying at the house for about seven months. He has been removed to the hospital, and the proprietor of the - house has been warned to keep the place closed to further lodgers for the present. The second case is that of an inmate of the Runcorn Union Workhouse. He recently visited the same lodging-house and returned to the Workhouse. He was on Sunday to be suffering from the disease, and he was removed to the small-pox hospital at Moore. The Workhouse has been closed.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[By A CORRESPOND EN r].

I am very pleased to be able to chronicle the fact that Mr. William Cooke, 45, Waterloo-road, Runcorn, son of Mr. Thomas Cooke, relieving officer, has been admitted a Fellow of the Royal College of Organists after exam- ination. As is well known, the tests are of a most searching character, and Mr. Cooke is to be congratu- lated for conferring this distinguished honour upon the town. In view of the increasing interest that is being taken in music, thel following particulars will prove interesting :—The Diploma states: "We, the Board of examiners appointed by the Royal College of o:ganislx, hereby certify that we have duly examined Mr. William Cooke in the theofy and practice of music according to the highest standards adopted for the College examina- tions and we declare him to be fully qualified to exer- cise the profession and duties of an organist." The examiners were Sir George C. Martin, M.V.0., Mus. Doc., St. Paul's Cathedral; C. J. Frost, Esq., Mus. Doe., St. Peter's Church, Brockley ; C. H. Lloyd, Esq., M.A., Mus. Doc., Eton College; W. S. Hoyte, Esq., F.R.C.0., All Saints', Margaret-street, Londpn; E. H. Turpin,Esq., Mus. Doc., London; H. A. 'Harding, Esq., Mus. Doc., Bedford, and C. W. Pearce, Esq., Mus. Doc., chairman.

The examination is practical and theoretical, consist- ing of a performance on the organ, of Bach's Prelude wad Fugue in D major; Allegro Moderato in A by Dr. Hopkins, and Gustave Merkel's Introduction and Fugue in G major. The other requirements at the organ were accompanying a psalm, playing from a vocal score at sight, as also an organ composition at sight, extemporiz- ing on a given subject, and the harmonizing of a melody. The paper work comprised exercises in Florid Counterpoint, a Fugal exposition, the arranging of a muSical excerpt, for full orchestra, the harmonization of melodies, and of an unfigured Ground Bass, and mis- cellaneous questions on musical history, form, organ construction, acoustics, orchestration, choir training,&c. Mr. Cooke was prepared for the examination by Dr. J.C. Bridge, M.A., organist of Chester Cathedral. Mr. Cooke is organist and choirmaster of All Saints' Church, Sutton Oak, St. Helens, and formerly held a similar appointment at St. Paul's Church, Widnes.

It is satisfactory to note that the Penrhyn Choir raised over £5O by their week end concerts at the Public I-rall. Mr. S. Taylor and Mr. F. J. Norman pre- sided at the concerts, both gentlemen referring to the origin of the struggle and hoping that some means would be found to bring about a settlement.

The small-pox has actually broken out at -Runcorn and at Dutton Workhouse. As far as can be ascertained at time of writing there have been no further develop- ments. The authorities appear to have taken every precaution to prevent the spread of the disease and it can only be hoped that their efforts may prove successful.

An interesting discussion in connection with the small-pox case at the Workhouse took place at the Board of Guardians meeting held at the Daresbury Sessional House on Tuesday. It was made quite clear that at the time MF. Cooke gave the man an order to the house he was in ignorance of the fact that the man had come from the lodging house in Fryer-street re- cently visited by a person suffering from small-pox. The Urban Council are to be asked to notify Mr. Cooke of any further outbreaks that may take place.

The Halton and Weston water supply is still under consideration, and the Urban Council are to 'be asked by the Rural Council to furnish certain particulars con- sidered necessary. The question is by no means finished with yet.

The committee appointed to consider the plans of the proposed Isolation Hospital near Dutton seem to be very favourably impressed with one which would cost £6,600. The hospital is really a necessity and, when additional details are forthcoming from the architect, tho plans will no doubt be passed and the work speedily commenced.

NO. BROWN. Borough TremorPr

long in the annals of the Runcorn Club. It was a magnificent struggle from start to finish; and there can be no doubt that the home team were the superiors. At

half-time Bradford led by a score that seeined impos- sible to equalise, and the hopes of Runcorn's supporters were down at zero. But the visitors fell away in a most unexpected manner, while the Linnets set ,about their work in workmanlike fashion, with the result that the Bradford contingent were smitten hip and thigh. The two penalty goals of the visitors were splendidly placed and were beauti- ful kicks. In the front rank Bradford were the superior—a defect more than counterbalanced by the additional smartness of the home backs. Houghton was a trifle eff form, but on the whole played a good game. Jolley, Jones, Richardson and Butterworth were the pick of a thoroughly hard-working combined team.

Quite a scare has been occasioned in Earlestown by the outbreak of small-pox, one case being reported to the authorities on Tuesday. It is, however, described as of a modified type, still every precaution is being taken to prevent the contagion spreading. Until the patient can be removed to an Isolation Hospital, the hous3 where he resides has been placed in quarantine, and nobody but the Sanitary Inspector is allowed to v;sit it. The patient paid a visit to Bolton about a fortnight ago, and it is supposed he contracted disease whilst there. He, however, never suspected, when he began to sicken, that he was affected with the com- plaint, but continued at his work until Saturday last, and even on Saturday night attended a meeting of Druids, of which he is secretary. On Sunday he was worse and had to remain in bed, and on Monday medical aid was summoned, but it was not until Tuesday that the case WAS properly diagnosed as small-pox, and then the trouble began.

The District Council had no place to remove him to, and application to various towns where there are Isola- tion Hospitals showed a disinclination on the part of the authorities to receive the case. What to do with the man the District Council are at a loss to know, but they are stirring themselves into the matter to secure some temporary accommodation. It is to be hoped, however, for the sake of the inhabitants, that no further cases will arise, otherwise the result may be serious. I suppose an Isolation Hospital will at last be found a necessity, and every effort will be made to erect one without delay.

DEATH OF MRS. CHARLES HARE-

HURST, RUNCORN.

Our readers will regret to hear of the death, which took place on Sunday afternoon, of Mrs. Cnarles Hazle- hurst, of Halton Grange, Runcorn. The deceased lady who was in her 72nd year, had been in failing health for some time and had been attended by Dr. McDougall. Her 'final illness dated from about a month ago. She was connected with two of the oldest and first families in Runcorn. He father, the late Mr. Philip Whiteway, was a leader in the public life of the town for many years, and her husband, the la:.e Mr. Charles Hazlehurst, occupied an equally high position. He was one of the proprietors of Messrs. Hazlehurst, and Sons' Soap Works, founded by his father, Mr. Thomas Hazlehurst, sen., and now owned by the United Alkali Company. The two families ware long and intimately associated with the principal industries of the town, and had a high reputation throughout tle district. The late Mrs. Hazlehurst always took a deep interest in the affairs of the town, and the extent of her unostentatious philanthropy will probably never be known. She gave freely to all deserving institutions' and the town is greatly the poorer by her death. She attended the Parish Church for many years; and was a staunch sup- ported of all the good work done both here and at Holy Tririty Church. Her death is generally deplored. When the news was received the flag over Messrs. Hazle- hurst's works was hung at half-mast. The deceased lady leaves three children, Mr. Charles Whiteway Hazle- hurst, Miss Julia Ann Hazlehurst, and Mrs. Edward.V. Rayner. ' THE FUNERAL.

On Wednesday the remains were interred in the family vault at Runcorn Cemetery. The vault had been specially decorated with white flowers and evergreens. Service was conducted in the church by the Rev. Canon A. M. Wood assisted by the Rev. R. C. Whiteway. The Parish Church Choir was in attendance and sang Psalm xc., and the hymn "Lead kindly light." At the grave- side the concluding rites were performed by the Vicar, and the choir gave a beautiful rending of the hymn "Now the labourer's task is o'er." The solemn cere- mony was witnessed by hundreds of spectators. Among the friends present were :—Messrs. J. W. Lightburn, S. Taylor. J.P.. W. Handley, F. J. Norman, J.P.,:S. Pope, J. R. Frith, A. Lightburu, J. H. Weston, W. Grice, F. Beck, S. Percival, R. Collinge, R. W. Harrop, R. Morris, 'Tames Lea, D. H. Jones, J. Horsfleld, R. Collinge, Charles Kirkham (Aston), James Mort, G. Pritchard, B. Savage, W. Shaw (Mersey Mission to Sea- men). W. Hankinson, E. Gregory, G. Brnuelow, William and Charles Bell, W. H. Chadwick, G. Acton, W.Sterton, J. Speed, C. Hardy, Heesom, Moss, Carter, the Revs. A. Wells, C. R. Killick, F. S. Thetford, J. Unsworth aud Greenhalgh, H. R. Carter, Dr. Robinson. . The chief mourners included Major Hazlehurst (son), Messrs. G. Lloyd Wigg, E. V. Rayner (son-in-law). C. Ha zlehnrst jun., E. Wigg (grandsons), E Rayner' C. Pink, Dr. McDougall. Rev. R. C. Whiteway. and Rev. AV. S. Scholes, Messrs. Tom Shaw, W. W. Gossage, E. FOOTBALL.

• A more miserable day for-football could not very well be imagined than last Saturday. Rain and sleet fell in torrents, and as the afternoon advanced, the wind commenced to blow strongly, which made matters infinitely worse. Nevertheless very fair crowd of enthusiasts put in an appearance to witness the encounter between Earlestown and Workington. Fancy travelling all the way -from Workington to wallow for an hour and a half in a veritable slough of despond. What the players thought about it I don't know, but the spectators derived any amount of amusement from the impromptu actions of the players. Football there really was none, but Earles- town who played down hill, peppered away at the Workington goal, the custodian of which was for the occasion invincible. Try as they would the Earlestown players could not get the ball past him, although they had numerous tries. On one occasion the Workington goal-keeper in saving a shot, fell with the ball in his hands, andalthough a crowd of players were round him, all trying to kick the ball into the net, he refused to loose his hold, and finally threw the ball away and it was cleared. It was a marvellous piece of work.

In the second half of the game Earlestownhad to face the hill, and the wind having risen,they had a double disadvantage,the rain and sleet blowing in their faces. Darkness came on rapidly and it was surprising that the referee did not stop the game. The players pegged away,but neither side scored until about 20 minutes off time, when Workington scored easily and a few minut3s afterwards scored a second paint. Finally with about 10 minutes still to play the referee stopped the game owing to the darkness.

To-morrow (Saturday), Earlestown are at home to Staleybridge Rovers, and it is hoped for this occasion they will have a fine day. A new goal keeper, a full back, and a forward on the left wing are being tried, anatwo points should certainly be added to Earlestown's poor record.
