THE PLACE OF MEETING.

The Chairman said that the circumstances which had brought them together in that building were to be very much deplored. He was sure they would all join in an expression of sympathy with the Master of the Work- house, and his family, and also the inmates. He had been making inquiries and had been told that the small-pox case that had broken out in the house constituted a grave danger. The man had been in an affected condition for days before he was pronounced affected. That made the case still more serious as the men's department in the House was in a very crowded condition. He prayed that the scourge would pass away without making any further ravage for they all knew what a dreadful disease it was. At the last meeting of the Council in thanking the officials for taking such prompt measures he omitted to mention the name of Dr. McDougall. He was glad to learn that as soon as he knew of the danger he took all the necessary steps.

THE INMATES TREAT POSTPONED.

Mr. Norm-an said he understood that the treat by the ladies to the inmates would now have to be postponed. The Clerk replied that the ladies' committee had already been notified that the treat would have to be postponed.

SMALL-PDX IN THE WORKHOUSE.

Mr. Norman said ii;ai,-re;;:tiiWi";ibe-;".;13 of small- pox he hurried to the meeting of the House Committee to gather information. Could the clerk give any in- formation as to the exact state of the House and what was being done at Dutton regarding the inmates? The Clerk in reply read the following report : The Rock, Frodsham, January 10th. 1903.—Dear sir,— I beg to inform you that a case of small-pox has been discovered in the Workhouse to-day, such case having been contracted about December 26th at Wooditne's lodging-house at Runcorn, where the patient (Samuel Walker) had slept. I saw the patient off to Moore Smallpox Hospital about 4 p.m. to-day. Have had all

contaCt cases iaccinated, mid taken every other pre- caution possible. It will not be advisable to have the ladies' entertainment on the 14th inst., and all dis- charges from, andall visitors to the Workhouse must be stopped for a period of 18 days.—l an yours faithfully, T. J. SELBY, M.B. Mr. Ashton, clerk. January Ilth, 1903. Dear sir,—Enclosed please find copies of letter to Local Government Board and also of instructions for the prevention and spread of small-pox. Froni the letter,you will see that the Workhouse has been placed in quarantine for 18 days. This will prevent your Board meeting there, and also any entertainment being given by the Ladies' Committee. Trusting that the steps taken will have the desired effect of stamping it out at the Workhouse to the satisfaction of us all.— Yours truly T. J. SELBY, M.B. Mr. Ashton. clerk. To the President of the Local Government Board. London. January 10th, 1903. Sir.—l have the honour to report that a case of small-pox has occurred to an inmate of Dutton Workhouse in the person of Samuel Walker, aged 59 years, who has been removed to the Small-pox Isolation Hospital at Moore. All persons who have been in contact with this infected patient have been re-vaccinated, the bedding destroyed, and all necessary precautions taken to prevent the spread of this disease. The source of infection appears to have been acommon lodging-house in Fryer-street, Runcorn, where the patient stayed on the nightof December 26th, 1902.-1 have the honour to be sir' your obedient servant, THOS. J. SELBY.—Appended to this letter was a precis of the various preventive precautions taken. The Clerk said he had already advertised that visitors would not be allowed at the Workhouse. Mr. Mawdsley asked why was the infected man sent to the House? Did they think he was ill?

Mr. Cooke, relieving ollicer, said that the man was one of those Who came in and out of the House continually. He made application on Christmas Day to :to to the HOILSO mid wastold to call again. He gave him an order on Saturday following and he was put in the receiving ward, afterwards being taken to the interior of the House.

Mr. Roylance asked whether the disease manifested itself after his being removed in the interior? Mr. Cooke replied that at the time he made the order he did not know that the smallpox case at St. Helens had been traced to the house in Fryer-street. He after- wards heard that the man had been traced to Weston and the week following he heard that the man had been traced to Fryer-street, lodging-house. He did not know that Walker had been to the lodging-house. He had since refused orders to the House and sent one case to Dr. McDougall. _ _

Mr. rifawdsley asked whether the people had been vaccinated at Weston Point tint not at Runcorn? Now the people were in such, a plight the best thing to do was to let Mr. Cooke and the other officers know of any outbreak to prevent suspicious characters being sent to the Workhouse.

The Clerk pointed out that such a notification in the Runcorn area would have to go through the Runcorn Urban Council..

After further discussion, Mr. Mawdsley moved that the Urban Council be asked to notify Mr. Cooke of any fresh outbreaks.

Mr. Roylance seconded and the resolutionwas passed. Mr. Norman said that it was satisfactory to know so much was being done to stamp out the disease. The origin of small-pox was filth, and the man Walker was a likely person to contract such disease. He would like to see a law inaugurated to make it an indictable offence for a man not to be clean.

ATTEMPTED SUICIDE AT RUNCORN.

• At the Runcorn Police Court on Wednesday, Ellen Done, married woman, living in Penketh-lane, was charged, before Mt. E. Imison, with having attempted to commit suicide on New Year's Day, by throwing herself into the Bridgewater Canal near the Sprinch- yard, Runcorn.—The evidence showed that a boatman saw the woman walking about the canal with a bundle in her arms. After sitting on the bank about five minutes she threw herself into the canal. He gave the alarm and Joseph Harrison, foreman of postal tele- graphs, jumped into the canal and brought the woman to the side where she was brought out. A blue quilt was seen floating in the canal, and on 133ing drawn to the side a baby was found underneath.—The baby was taken to the Police Station, where Mrs. Ennion res- tored it to consciousness and the woman was taken home. On being asked why she had done such a thing, the woman replied that her husband had said the child was not his.—Prisoner was committed to the Quarter Sessions for trial, bail being allowed, herself in £lO and one surety of £5.

A CANAL BOAT TRAGEDY NEAR RUNCORN.

A shocking affair is reported from Sutton Dock near Runcorn. it appears that Mrs. Ann Spencer, a widow, is the captain, and her niece Maggie Gough, a single woman, aged 23 years, is the mate of the barge Chance It, belonging to the Salt Union; Ltd., which carries coal from Sutton Dock, belonging to Mesers. W. Carroll and Sons, of Liverpool and Winsford. On Sunday night the barge was moored at the wharf. At half-past five on Monday morning Gough got up to fetch the horse from the stables, and she did not return. Her aunt afterwards went in search of her niece, and she found her sitting on a stone near the stables. The un- fortunate young woman confessed that she had given birth to a child on the deck, of an adjoining flat,and that she had thrown the baby into the Weaver Canal. She was in a pitiable condition, and was taken back to the cabin of the Chance It, and later in the day was appre- hended by Police-sergeant Bennett, and conveyed to Dutton Workhouse. Efforts to recover the body of the infant were unsuccessful up to Thursday afternoon.

===== 0002601_19030116_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL

COMPANY.'

(Continued from page 6.)

Mr. Justice Byrne resumed the hearing of the action on Thursday. THE SPECIAL TRIBUNAL QUESTION.

Mr. Justice Byrne, referring to Mr. Moulton's objec- tion on behalf of the defendants to the jurisdiction of the Court, on the ground that the Act of 1885 provided that matters of difference should be dealt with by an engineer appointed by the Board of Trade, said this was an extremely important point for both parties. Which- ever way he decided it, the losing parties would prob- ably not be satisfied with his decision, but would take the matter to the Court of Appeal. In that view of the case he did not see the use of detaining the witnesses and suggested that that point having been decided the rest of the case should be adjourned pending the decision of the Court of Appeal. Mr. Bousfield, K.C., said that if his Lordship decided in the plaintiffs' favour he was entitled to go on. Mr. Justice Byrne said that as the defendants said they were not satisfied with his decision and wished to take the opinion of another tribunal,he thought it would be better for the plaintiffs to have the hearing of the action postponed. Mr.Busfield said his lordship would deal with the matter as he thought best, having regard to the rights of the parties. Proceeding to deal with Mr. Moul- ton's preliminary objection, he contended that the de- fendant having pat in a statement of defence without, rais:ng the point as to 'reference to an engineer ap- pointed by the Board of Trade, had waived their rights under the original Act. WhaLever the rights of the de- fendants were, he submitted that the objectioncould not be taken after the action had begun, and that the Court in the present juncture of affairs could nok, make the amendments proposed by his friend. Dealing with the objection thak, individual traders could not sue, he con tended that the action was properly constituted for the purpose for which it was intended, namely, the protec- t:on of the traders who were plaintiffs, because they had a common right to ask that during the time the eanal Comp Any was in default they should go free of tolls down the Canal.

Mr. Norton, K.C., following on the same side, con- tended that it was not the Corporation of Warrington but the surveyor to the Corporation who had to say whether or not the river bed was being properly dredged. Whatever might be the rights of the Warrington Cor- poratioa, there was nothing in the'Act which said that the traders were to go to arbitration at all. By section 88 the traders were given an independent right to certain rates of navigation and other things, which they could enforce by action. The • traders must have a right of their own, because in some cases their interests were antagonistic to those of the Coporation. Mr. Justice Byrne, interposing, said the Court had jurisdiction to send a case to arbitration if it thought it was a fit case, and he must say he should have thought it far better that this case should have gone to arbitration:

Mr. Moulton, K.C., asked that this action might be dismissed, because the plaintiffs had come to the wrong tribunal. The Legislature had appointed a special tribunal to try these disputes between the Corporation of Warrington and the Ship Canal Company, and had it in such a way as to exclude the courts unless there was a joint agreement. Wr. Justice Byrne said he would give his opinion on the preliminary objection on Friday morning.

OUTBREAK OF SMALL-PDX AT EARLESTOWN.

On Tuesday a case of small-pox was notified to the authorities at Earlestown. The patient is Mr. G. F. Parker, residing at 35, Haydock-street, Earlestown, and employed at the Viaduct Works aa a lifter. He had been to Bolton a little over a week ago, and two or three days after his return began to feel tmwell. He, however, endeavoured to overcome the feeling and on Saturday night last attended a meeting at the Griffin Hotel. On Sunday he was much worse and at last had to summon Dr. Valentine, who at first thought he was suffering from chicken-pox. Developments came quickly however, and on Tuesday morning the symptoms were such as to leave no doubt of the case being one of small-pox. His isolation was at once ordered, but a difficulty presents itself,as the Council have no hospital, and neighbouring towns, where there is an infectious disease hospital, refused to receive him. The residence of the man has been put in quarantine,and the authorities would like to gethold of the men who came in contact with Parker on Saturday night at the Druid's meeting.

VIADUCT WORKS OFFICE STAFF ANNUAL DINNER. On Saturday the annual dinner of the Viaduct Works office staff,to which the foremen were invited, took place at the "Lisboa," Victoria-street, Liverpool. The party travelled to Liverpool by the 1-9 p.m.. train,. a saloon having been proved for their acconnuodation. On arriving they dispersed to pass the intervening time before dinner in various ways, several visiting the LiveQoof Football ground to witness the game there wi,h Sunderland. Dinner was served at 6 o'clock, and proved do be of an excellent character, the diners thoroughly enjoying the good things provided. On the removal of the cloth the proceedings were presided over by Mr. T. Ormand. The toast of" The King," "Prince of Wales," "The L. and N.-W. Railway Company and the Superintendent of the Waggon Deparanent "were proposed and duly honoured, the latter being suitably responded to by Mr. C. W. Emmett. The other toasts were "The Office Staff,"" The Foremen," each of which were duly honoured. The musical items were very well rendered. " Life is a river" and "The Arab's farewell to his steed," by Mr. J. Owen; "Queen of the Earth," by Mr. J. Swann ; "The sea is England's glory "and "The Village Blacksmith," Mr. J. Wadding- , ton ; and" Our Jack's come home," Mr. C. W. Emmett. The singing of "Auld Lang Syne," brought a very 1 pleasant gathering to a close.

IMEARLESTOWN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY EMPLOYEES. The employs of the Co-operative • Society had a social gathering in the Co-operative Hall on Monday evening. The object of calling them together was for the purpose of explaining fully the working of a new system of checks which is about to be introduced. An excellent knife and fork tea was provided, something like 100 persons partaking of it. After tea Mr. D. Forrester, president of the society, fully explained the details of the system, and the evening was afterwards enjoyably spent in singing, dancing, and games.

WARRINGTON.

Personal. We regret to state that Mr. Charles Jackson Holmes is confined to his house, Barnacre, Grappenhall suffer- ing from an internal complaint. Mr. and Mrs. Ziba Armitage will leave England for the North of Italy on the 20th inst. - H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING OF Tosics.--63, SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. Death of an Ex-Police Inspecton Mr. William James, formerly chief inspector in the Warrington Police Force, died at his residence in Padgate-lane at five o'clock on Thursday morning at the age "of: 70. He was connected with the Police Force for over 31 years, joining it in July, 1865, as a constable. He was promoted to the merit class in 1869, and the following year was made a sergeant. He was appointed inspector in 1875. and in 1884 he was promoted to the rank of Chief Inspector of the Force. In 1896 he retired on his pension.

ASK for CUNN. INGHAM'S HIGH-CLASS MINERAL WATERS.—Advt. Another Smallpox Case at St. Helens._______

On Thursday afternoon a man named William Darlington, aged about fifty-two, who had been stay- ing at Grimshaw's Lodging-house, Salisbury-street, applied to the relief offices for medical aid, and was sent to Dr. Fox, who at once certified that he was suffering from smallpox. The sanitary authorities quickly removed him to hospital, and took steps to have the inmates of the lodging-house vaccinated. This. is the seventh case that has arisen in the town.

OVERCOME BY GAS AT EARLESTOWN.

On Saturday a man named Hilton, 40 years Of age, in the employ of the Newton UrLan District Counc:l, was enga:.;ed at the openlng of gt.s mains nee: the Wargrave inn, Earlestown, when he was ove:•come by an escape of gas, and w‘ts mattered unconscious. He was carried into the Wargrave inn, and Dr. Li.:-ham, who was sum- moned, succeeded in restoring the man. He is now nearly well again. GAS WORKERS' DINNER AT EARLESTOWN.

The fi,•st annual dinner promoted by the -e-mpit,y4s at the Newton-in-Makerfield U.D.C. Gas Works was held at the Oak Tree Inn, on Saturday. About 40 sat down to an excellent repast provided by Mr. and Mrs. H. Toft. After dinner, the evening was passed in singing, Stc.,Mr. A. Bowers (the gas engineer) occupying the chair. Re- sponding to the toast of his health, &c., the chairman said that they were all very anxious to make the gas works a successful undertaking, and one of which they might be proud. Owing to reasons which he would not go into, the gas works had not been as successful during the last two or three years as might have been wiched, but he believed that better days were in store for them. Great improvements had been made in the works and he trusted that others would follow in the near future. He was proud to be amongst them on this the first occasion of their annual dinner and he hoped to be present on similar occasions in years to come. Mr. Hoggard who acted as vice-chairman, after endorsing the chairman's remarks, said that he had worked in five different gasworks but he copld con- scientiously say that he knew of no gasworks which was better manned than that of Newton-in-Makerfield. The proceedings terminated in the usual manner.

INFLUENZA INCREASING.

The Influenza Epidemic is again spreading over the Country. The visitation is of a very prostrating nature, and the disease is certainly on the increase. The origin of the disease or rather the circumstances which bring it about is as much a mystery as ever. You cannot trifle with Influenza. It undermiues the Nervous System to a greater extent than almost any other disease, and it is Pneumonia that kills, and Pneumonia strikes the man who imagines he has only a mild form of Influenza, and can go about the street just as usual. It can hardly be doubted that the prevalence of Chest and Lung Complaints must be ascribed to the baneful influence of Influenza, whose after-effects so frequently take this form. It was a bad day for this Country when this malignant scourge first visited our shores. Year after year it has never since failed to revive at its due time, and to extort its tribute of human life. Having observed its ravages, and its dreadful effects upon the constitution on the occasion of its former visits, we cannot but regard its recurrence with much fear, as a great many have not yet, if they ever will,regain their former health. As a preventative, we know of nothing equal to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. A few strong doses taken in time have often provedeffectual in ward- ing off attacks of this Malady, and always succeed in mitigating their severity when a patient is under their influence. Do you suffer from Chills, alternating wity Sudden Flushes. Severe Head-ache, accompanied bh difficulty of breathing, and symptoms of Catarrh, Pains in the Chest, under and between the shoulders, and a stiffness and sbreness of the Muscles, Loss of Appetite and Nervousness. If you suffer from any of these symp- toms, know that they are the forerunners of Influenza, and it behoves you to resort at once to a course of Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. By common consent Patients everywhere admit that they feel more depressed and miser- able after an attack of Influenza than while under its influence, and more than they feel after an attack of any other illness. The best way to banish these miser- able feelings is to take Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, The Vegetable Tonic. We can strongly and confidently recommend this preparation as it has treated severe cases of Influenza, Heavy preparation, Extreme Weakness, and Langour, with greater success than any other known Remedy. We may also add that as a Remedy for Indi- gestion, Sleeplessness, Low Spirits, Nervousness. and Loss of Appetite, Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters stands unequalled. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

A HEARTLESS WARRINGTON FATHER.--CHILDRMi HAD TO EAT REFUSE. Sentence of a month's imprisonment was passed upon Thomas Baxter, a respectably dressed man living in David-street, at the Warrington Police Court on Tues- day for neglecting his six children. Mr. Crompton, who prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., said the prisoner was form2rly engaged as barman at Messrs. Pickmere's, Bridge-street, but lost his position through his own conduct. He then became an insurance agent, and also lost that situation. Since September last he had done practically nothing to maintain his wife and family. Whatever money he earned he spent upon him- self or in drink. He left his children without any food and they had to go out begging. The prisoner not only permitted them to do this, but even ate the food the children begged. Some kind person gave one of the children the remnants of a chicken, and the child took it home. When the prisoner came in he took possessign of the food and did not give the children any. On one occasion, when the children were crying for food, the prisoner, who had money in his possession, sent out for some whisky. Inspector Hackett had visited the house, and warned prisoner. During the month prior to October 15th the prisoner had given his wife only 13s. 6d. When the inspector visited the house at eleven o'clock one morning the prisoner was in bed. On the 10th of November, Inspector Hackett and Mr. Kane, assistant school attendance officer, found the children crying for food. and one of them came into the house eating a piece of vegetable refuse, which had been picked up out of an ashpit. It was a common practice for the children to search the ashpits at the back for something to eat. The prisoner went out "dressed up." Most of the clothing which the children were now wearing was borrowed. The society had p_rovided food for them on many occasions. Evidence bearing out this statement was given, prisoner's wife stating that he generally, got up about dinner time.—School Attendance Inspector Kane said the case was the most persistent and heartless case he had ever known. The children were continually begging, and were half starved. Prisoner had been fined for not sending them regularly to school. He saw one of the children eating a piece of cabbage stalk which had been picked up in the street and was covered with mud. —Prisoner said it was entirely his wife's fault that he had lost his situations. When he was at Pickmere's she used to "kick up bother," and while he was an insur- ance agent she rifled his pockets, and he got his accounts wrong. Hehad done his best for the children. —The Bench considered the case fully proved,and sent prisoner to gaol for a month.

A WARRINGTON MAN'S INGENIOUS FRAUD. fore At the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, be Mr. W. Long, and other magistrates Thomas Monks, Robson-street. Warrington, was charged on remand with having on the 30th December, at Antrobus, en- deavoured to obtain by false pretences from Samuel Wainwright, farmer, the sum of 358. with intent to defraud.—Mr. Steel defended.—Nhe prosecutor said that on the day in question the prisoner came to his house, Moss Side Farm, Antrobus, and told him that his (the complainant's) brother, who lived in London had been charged with travelling from the city to Witton station without a ticket, and had been locked up by the magis- trates. The brother could only find 10s., and unless the remainder of the money was paid by seven o'clock that evening he would be removed to Knutsford Gaol and that would entail additional cost: The prisoner added that, he had been asked by the inspec- tor of the station to come to complainant for the money. Witness dealined to part with the money until he had seen his brother, and he and the prisoner started to walk to NorthwApolice station. Inquiries were made both at the railway station and the police station, and it was found that the prisoner's statement was quite untrue. The inspector at the station denied having seen the prisoner or having sent him to the prose- cutor.—Prisoner gave evidence and said that as he was subject to epileptic fits he was unable to obtain regular employment. On the 30th December he went to North- wich to seek employment. He was standing outside the station waiting for Fairclough's cart to come up to take him back to Warrington. While doing so a railway inspector came to him and asked him to go to Antrobus and tell prosecutor that his brother had been locked up for travelling from London to Witton station without a ticket. After the inspector had shown him the way to Antrobus he promised to go to the prosecutor for the money and did so,telling him the story that the inspector had told him. On his oath, he said in answer to Mr. Steel, that he had not attempted to obtain the money by false pretences .t as God was his judge."—Sergeant Bourne said he was at the Petty Sessions at Daresbury, on tbe 30th April,l9ol, when the defendant was sent to gaol for a month with hard labour for obtaining money by false pretences at Grappenhall. —The Bench sent prisoner to gaol for two months with hard labour.

WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL COMPANY.

THE DREDGING OF THE MERSEY. Big Action by the Corporation and

Traders.

I In the Chancery Division of the High Court of Justice on Tuesday Mr. Justice Byrne began the hearing of an action brought by Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons, Limited, against the Manchester Ship Canal Company. The plaintiffs claim—(l) A declaration that:the defendant company have since April 9, 1896, been, and now are, by virtue of the Manchester Ship Canal Acts, 1885 to , 1896, and the agreement of the 9th April, 1896, bound at their own cost to dredge the beds and banks of the river Mersey, and to maintain the same dredged so that there shall at all times be Bft. of water at low I water at sping tides in the channel between thewestern boundary of the works of Monks, Hall, and Co., at or near Bank Quay and Howley Lock, including a portion ,of the old bed of the river Mersey; (2) a declaration that in the period during which the defendant Company have failed or shall continue to fail to discharge their obligations, the traders of Warrington possess the right of navigation with respect of vessels passing through the Walton Lock of the portion of the Manchester Ship Canal extending from Eastham to the Walton Lock and of the portion of the river Mersey referred to, free of all tolls and dues ; (3) an order on the defendant Com- pany to repay the traders and manufacturers all dues and tolls paid since the 9th April, 1896, in respect of the navigation of the Ship Canal between the limits referred to ' • (4) damages for breach by the defendant Company of the obligations cast on them by the agree- mAnt and statutes; (5) an injunction to restrain the defendant Company from committing further breaches of the agreement; (6) and costs. Mr. Bousfield, K.C., Mr. Hughes, K.C., Mr. Norton, K.C., and Mr. Thomp- son appeared for the plaintiffs, and Mr. Moulton. K.C., Mr. Cripps, K.C., Mr. Pickford. K.C, and Mr. 0. Leigh Clare for the defendants. After an opening statement by Mr. Bousfleld the inquiry was adjourned. THE CASE FOR THE PLAINTIFFS. _ _ _

On WG:cinesday, Mr. Bousfleld, continuing his opening of the case, said the plaintiffs, Joseph Crosfleld and Sons, James Fairclough and Sons, and Monks, Hall, and Co., were traders and manufacturers at Warrington, and the remaining plaintiffs were the mayor, aldermen, and bur- gesses of the Borough. On the 12th December, 1893, the defendant Company undertook to complete the dredging of the Mersey at Warrington required by section 88 of their Act of 1885 by July, 1894. The agreement also contained provisions enabling the traders of Warrington to carry goods and materials free of rates along certain portions of the canal watil the dredging was completed. The Company did not dredge the river to a proper depth by the Ist July, 1894, nor had they ever done so, and from the date of the agreement until 1895 the traders used the canal within the limits referred to,free of tolls. On the 9th April, 1896, a further agreement was made between the the plaintiffs and the efendant Company. By clause 1 it was agreed that sub-section 14 of the Act of 1885 should be read as if it were in the words following : "(a) The Company shall forthwith at their own cost, dredge the bed and banks of the river Mersey and main- tain it dredged so that there shall be a depth of Bft. of water in the channel between the wyetern boundary and the works of Monks, Hall, and OM., at or nerr Bank Quay and Howley Lock; (b) the Company shall not be bound to dredge the banks of the Mersey or maintain the depth of Bft of water unless and until the locks at Howley and Twenty-steps Bridge are enlarged so as to enable vessels of increased size to get to Howley, in which event the Company shall be bound at their own cost to dredge the bed and banks of the Mersey be- tween the works of Monks, Hall, and Co., and the eastern boundary of the borough of Warrington; (c) with regard to so much of the Mersey as passes nude Warrington Bridge the Company may at that point re- duce the minimum width of 50 feet at the bottom of th channel to a not greater extent than may be unavoid- able." By clause 4 the Company bound themselves* "effectually to execute and complete at their own cost the several dredging and other works prescribed by the agreement." In the early part of 1897 the defendant Company claimed payment of dues from the traders and manufacturers of Warrington for vessels which had been using their canal, alleging that as from the date of the agreement of 189 G the traders were not entitled to free navigation of the canal, and p tyments had from that date been made by the traders to the canal company in respect of such dues under protest. Notices had from time to time been given to the defendant Company,ca,ll- - attention to the fact that they had failed to make the channel as provided by the agreements and statutes or done the dredging properly. The Company, had, however, never remedied the matters complained of,but they insisted on payment of dues and tolls from the traders of Warrinocrton. Counsel then read the lengthy correspondence which passed between the parties before the action was set down.

A SPECIAL TRIBUNAL,

Mr. Moulton, interposity, on behalf of the Manchester Ship Canal Company, said he had a preliminary objec- tion. He submitted the court had no jurisdiction to deal with the questions raised in the action,because o the provisions of clause 83 of tke Act of 1885 and thf arbitration clause in the agreement of 1890. He pointee out that the Act of 1835-provided that if any differencd should arise it should he determined by an engineers who was to be appointed (on the application of either of the parties) by the Board of Trade, whose decision should be final and binding on both parties and the costs of the reference should be borne as he should direct. This was a statutory direction that matters of difference should be determined by a special tribunal, and they could therefore only be determined by that special tribunal. Mr. Clare followed on the same side. Mr. Bousfleld, for the plaintiti-i, argued tint unless it was clear that the intention was that some special tribunal the jurisdiction of the court was not ousted. Counsel should take place of the ordinary tribunal aurged in his clients' interests at length, and quoted many authorities, and had not concluded at the rising of the court. The hearing was further adjourned.,

The Examiner, Friday, January 16, 1903

—but of my fellow Catholics generally. I hope I may I also add that this has been a result beyond your wishes ' and intention. I have received numerous expressions of indignation aroused by your remarks, not from Catholics only, but from others, including at least two of your fellow-magistrates, though I believe they were not present on the occasion. It seems to me, sir, that you have taken a most unfairadvantage of your position as a magistrate. (Hear, hear.) Your remarks are founded, and seem to accept and confirm the statements of a criminal whom you sentence to three months. You reject his defence of himself, yet accept his statements without hesitation or inquiry from others most nearly concerned when they seem to reflect on a Catholic priest. (Hear hear.) Do you, sir, consider that this was fair and judicial? Indeed the facts were very far from truthfully and correctly stated. Ontwo occasions, and on two only, did Dr. Mercer urge upon the man Cullen the conscientious obligation of reclaim- ing his child. (Hear, hear.) Six months at least elapsed between the last occasion and the retnrn of the child. You can judge for yourself, then' how griev- ously felt was that pressure put forward as a pretext to shelter himself from the consequence of his own: neglect. Again, Dr. Mercer, though he called as re- cently as New Year's Day, had not been informed of the child's return and consequently could not, though otherwise able and willing, have done anything for the welfare of the child. What we could have done of course we should have gladly done. At the same time knowing well the circumstances of the case I do not hesitate to say that it was not one in which private charity could have been prudently or profitably exercised. (Applause.) I can quite believe that the strong forces of the law might with advantage have been brought to bear upon the case at a much earlier stage. This explanation I offer merely to show how hasty and unfair you have allowed yourself to be. Do not imagine for a moment that I put it forth as an excuse or apology for the course taken by Dr. Mercer. (Applause.) It requires neither the one nor the other. I fully concur in it and accept its responsibilities. Catholics believe that the welfare of the soul must be preferred to and, if necessary, secured by the sacrifice of the welfare of the body. (Hear, hear.) We also believe that the soul's welfare is inseparably bound up with the profession dnd practice of our religion. Con- sequently, as in the case in point, where the child's material wellbeing is to be purchased by a sacrifice of its spiritual birthright the heritage of the Faith, no faithful priest or member of the Church could take any other course, than by all legitimate means to dissuade the parent from such a bargain. (Hear, hear.) This is in the domain of conscience and is the proper sphere of the priest's action which is consequently grossly mis- named by you as interference. (Hear, hear.) The principle just communicated is no new one, but one that has been acted on in all ages and witnessed to *rough many centuries of persecution and hostile legislation; and it is one, you may rest assured, that will never be relinquished either in theory or in practice by the faith- ful. Ido not suppose that this principle appeals to you I do not expect you to agree with us in this matter, but I did expect that you would have given due considera- tion to the well-known tenets of many millions all the world over, and to the feelings of thousands of your own fellow-citizens. I did expect that you would have , refrained at least when on the Bench from such utter- ances as must not only deeply wound the feelings of Catholics, but also shake their confidence in your sense of fairness.—l am, yours truly, NEVILLE WILSON." (Applause.) , To this Mr. Monks wrote :—" Walton Old Hall, near Warrington, 10th January. 1903. Dear Father Wilson, —I beg to acknowledgeyour letter of the 9th inst. and let me say at once that I have no wish or the slightest intention of being offensi ve,and I regret that you should feel annoyed or hurt at the cmnments upon the action of one of your priests. This, however, cannot be avoided at times in a free country—(laughter)—where we areall free to criticise not only each other's beliefs, but the conduct arising from a conscientious desire to enforce them. I should be very sorry to take an unfair advan- tage of my position as a magistrate, but we had plenty of evidence before us to the effect that the child had been removed in consequence of the pressure of Dr. I Mercer upon the parent—(hear, hear),—when Mrs. I Rylatt declined to consent to bring up the child as a ' member of the Roman Catholic Church. Mrs Rylatt , made her statement upon oath,and it was confirmed both t by the man Cullen and his sister,with the further remark I that he had regretted having done so ever since. Your: remarks as to your knowledge of the circumstances of j the family leading you to the conclusion that it was not a case where 'private charity could have beenprudently I or profitably exercised,' makes the insistence on the I child's remeval all the more cruel and unjust. I know i that the teaching of your Church insists upon the j children of Roman Catholic parents being brought up I in the Roman Catholic faith, bul have never known it ' put so plainly as in your letter. (Laughter.) But we Protestants denounce such teaching as unchristian. (Laughter.) It is subversive of parental authority, and an invasion of private rights which we would not submit to for a single day. Your conscience is domin- ated by your vow to obey the ecclesiastical authorities of your Church, and is, therefore, not free to take a course, however humane it may be, in opposition to its teachings, and the fact that 'it had been acted upon for ages, and witnessed to through many centuries of persecution and hostile legislation' is no evidence of its truthfulness, but on the contrary for centuries it has been felt to be an unjust and oppressive teaching which ought to be abolished. The fact that no other free church propounds such a theory, is conclusive that the majority of mankind are against you. Our right to criticise and condemn if we think proper, begins at once when the man and his family are thrown on to the public purse for maintenance: and if, as the result of your teaching, the authority of the Church is set above the authority of the law, it becomes a public danger against which we have to be upon our guard. Now, in all this please don't accuse me of wishing to hurt your feelings in the least. (Laughter.) You must recognize my right to state my case in my own way, and if I' venture to sharply criticise the position you assume in your letter to me, you must, at least, give me credit for as much sincerity as yourself. We shall not agree on many points of doctrine and church government, and we will , not discuss our differences, but we can agree to differ and recognize equal rights, and work together in all good work believing in that charity that thinketh no evil.— Yours faithfullly, F. MONKS." In answer to that he (Father Wilson) wrote :—" St. Mary's, Warrington, January 12th, 1903. Dear sir,—ln reply to yours of the 10th inst., while acknowledging its more courteous tone, I have to say_ that it is just because we live in a free country that I consider you have abused your position as a magistrate in your com- ments on the action of the Rev. Dr. Mercer. (Applause.) You seem to have fallen into a singular fallacy in your attempt to justify yourself in criticising , and condemning that action from the Bench. You more than insinuate that in some way or other—how you do not state—the law of the land was violated and the authority of the Church set above that of the State. I declare on the contrary that the action of the ' priest was merely an exercise of that just liberty which the laws of this free country secure to all, and of which,except in our case, you seem so justly proud. I quite understand your arguments and your position. Both areas old as Christianity—(laughter)— both were adopted by Nero and Diocletian—(renewed laughter)—who enforced them with logical severity to their bloody conclusion. You seem to consider as a dangerous people unless we allow ourselves to be false to our faith. (Hear, hear). Apparently you do not relish the laws which secure our liberties, but would prefer a legal suppression of our freedom. But are you not herein rather out of date, somewhat reactionary,far , behind the great majority of your more enlightened countrymen? At any rate, I think few will consider , it seemly that the magisterial bench should be made , the platform of such an agitation.—l am, yours truly, NEVILLE WILSON."

In reply to that he received that evening the follow- ing 'Monks, Hall and Co., Limited, Warrington, 13th January, 1903. Dear Father Wilson,—Your letter of the 12th to hand, and there is no fallacy or insinuation in the matter at all. It is all plain and painful fact. The man Cullen was brought up before us for breaking the law of the land in neglecting his children, one a child 12 months old, so shamefully that the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children prosecuted him for the offence after the child had been removed to the Workhouse Hospital to save it from further starvation and possible death. The solicitor proved the case, and produced evidence to show that in five months the child had been reduced from a fine healthy, well-cared-for child to the condition of a skeleton only weighing 811 b., and during that time its cries were so frequent and per- sistent as to call the attention of the neighbours to its condition, but without redress until they called the attention of the society's officer to it, and he took the necessary steps for its removal. The man's plea was that the priest insisted upon the child being removed from Mrs. Rylatt's care be6ause she would not consent to have the child brought up a Roman Catholic, and in consequence Mrs. Rylatt gave up the child, and it was handed back to the man Cullen, with the result I have stated. Now, all this cruelty to the child would have been avoided but for the action of Dr. Mercer, because the child would have remained under the fostering care of Mrs. Rylatt, therefore the priest is morally responsible for the cruelty to the child. ' (laughter.) You justify his action and accept the responsibility on the ground that your Church believes that the welfare of the nail must be preferred to and, if necessary, be secured even by the sacrifice of the welfare of the body. What is all that,but your attempt to put the Church above the law? I deny your assertion that the action of the priest was merely an exereise of that just liberty which the law of this free country secured to all.' I call it an abuse of that liberty, and it would soon be made criminal but for the hesitation English men have of making martyrs for conscience sake Laughter.) ===== 0002601_19030116_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
ROMAN CATHOLICS AN3 MR. F. MONKS. THE ALLEGED "PRIESTLY INTOLERANCE." Crowdd kleetig of Protest. LAITY DEFEND THE PRIEST.

Considerable indignation has been aroused among the Roman Catholics of Warrington by the remarks uttered by Mr. F. Monks, J.P., at the Borough Police Court during the hearing of a case of child neglect on Monday week. It will be remembered that Thomas Cullen, a labourer living in Wellington-street, was charged at the instance of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children with neglecting his six children. One of the children neglected was a baby 12 months old. It appeared that the mother died when it was a month old, and the father was left with the care of six children. A friend named Mrs. Rylatt offered to take charge of the baby, and the man agreed to this course. After six months had elapsed he insisted upon the baby being brought back, and the neglect for which he was sent to gaol took place. At the Police-court the prisoner stated that Dr. Mercer, Roman Catholic priest, insisted upon the return of the child, as Mrs. :Rylatt would not bring it up in the Roman Catholic faith. Mr. Monks,who was Chairman of the Bench,thereupon remarked that "this was a species of priestly intoler- ance that ought to be put an end to." In sentencing the man to three month's imprisonment Mr. Monks also remarked that he thought it was a most unfortunate thing that the priest interfered. He thought it was most reprehensible on the part of any minister or priest to interfere" in that sort of way, and advised the prisoner not to let the priest interfere any more than he could help in his household. Such was the feeling of Roman Catholics in the matter that a meeting of protest against the remarks, organized by the laity, was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Tuesday evening. Only men were admitted,And there was a crowdedattendance, Mr. George Vernon presiding. There were also present on the platform :—Councillor Brophy, and Messrs. C. H. Unsworth, E. A. Connor, J. Magian, Owen Toole,Rigby Eekersley, J. Caldwell, Austin Vernon, J. Richardson, Sexton, Rabbit, Kilduff, and Dr. Geary. The priests present were the Rev. Fathers Whittle, Wilson, Dr. Mercer, Darby and Duggan (Liverpool), Ryan, McGrath, D'Arcy, Baines, Gibbons, Swarbreck, Primavesi, Hind, and Jackson.—The proceedings throughout were very enthusiastic.

THE CHAIRMAN AND Milt. MONKS. "ATTACKING 10,000 CITIZENS OF WARRINGTON."

The CHAIRMAN in referring to the hearing of the case said that meeting had nothing to do with the sentence passed upon Cullen, the justice of which they did not doubt. What they had to find fault with, were certain remarks which were made by the presiding magistrate. In his opinion Mr. Monks deliberately went out] of his way to insult one of their priests. (Applause). No doubt Cullen to do the best for himself, was trying to play on the feelings of the magistrates, and with that object he brought in the name of one of their priests and tried to lay the blame or the greater part of it upon him,as the cause of the suffering of the child. He thought they would agree with him that there was no cause whatever why Mr. Monks should have arrived at the conclusion he did,and made the remarks he did. (Hear, hear.) That meeting was to protest most strongly against the language used by Mr. Monks—(applikuse)—and such remarks coming from a magistrate were, in his opinion, the usurpation of the office of a magistrate. (Applause.) Doubt- less they would have noticed on the day that the trial was held,the placards of the evening papers on which were the words in large letters: "Alleged intolerance. of a Catholic priest." ("Shame.") He noticed it, but at the same time he must confess it did not upset him. He put it down to the utterances of some "over zealous fanatic on the warpath:" (Applause.) But he was greatly surprised on coming to read the article to find the remarks had been uttered in the Borough Police Court by Mr. Monks. At the time it caused a great deal of talk and indignation among the Catholics in the town, and he was happy to say they had great sympathy from many of their fellow townsmen who differed from them in religious belief. (Hear, hear.) The matter was immediately taken up by the laity of the several congregations. He might say it was a spontaneous combustion. (Laughter.) He would not try to explain the theological aspect of the question, because he could not do so, but he wanted to say that the priest named in this case knew his duty—(hear, hear, and applause)— and it was sufficient to say he would do it. (Cheers.) What they were concerned with,was the unjustifiable attack on their priest. (Hear, hear.) The priests Were always open to attack, and they knew they were able to I defend themselves—(hear, hear)—but priests and people were bound together in solid bonds—(hear, hear)—and whoever attacked the priests attacked the people. (Applause.) Their fight was the people's fight, and the people were always ready to rally round their priests and to defend them as in duty bound. It was not, however, that they thought of duty altogether. It was a labour of love. (Hear, hear.) He thought the lives of their priests were too well known to need any defence whatever. (Hear, hear.) They spoke for themselves. On the battlefield, in the Ifever-stricken slums of their cities, in the fever hos- pitals and wherever there was danger, the priests were I almost first and foremost, ready to do their duty— (applause)—which they did do. They daily sacriqced their lives nobly on the altar of duty. It was not often that Catholics were roused. When attacks were made on their religion very often they passed them by as the words of bigots .and fanatics, but when attacks were made on their priests it was a different thing. They ought.to rouse themselves and let;people know that they were not living in the days of the Catacombs, but were living in the age of tolerance and the so-called enlighten- ed twentieth century. They ought to let them see that Catholics could not be attacked with impunity as they had hitherto been. In Warrington he supposed they had about 10,000 Catholics out of a population of 65,000. That was no mean quantity, and if Catholics were organized as the political clubs were, they would be able to make themselves felt in many ways---in Parliamen- tary and municipal elections and divers ways. (Applause). They didnot wish to "brag," but it was well to let the people of Warrington know their strength. If they were fighting and their adversary saw they were better armed and equipped than he was, he would think twice before he attacked. They would let Mr. Monks see that in attacking their priest he was attacking 10,000 citizens of Warrington. (Cheers.) They were present that night to pass a resolution of condemnation of the unjustifiable attack on their priest, and a copy of it would be sent to Mr. Monks for his persual They had come to speak in a friendly spirit and not to abuse anybody. He hoped all the speakers would bear that in mind. In conclusion Mr. Vernon read a letter from the St. Albans' Athletic Club tendering their sincere sympathy with Dr. Mercer and repudiating the "contemptible remarks" made by Mr. Monks. In the enlightened twentieth century, continued the letter, they looked to their local justices for something better than "ignorant slander." (Applause.) They considered the remarks totally out of place and they would like to see the local justices administer the law in a simple and straight- forward manner. (Applause.) He asked Father Wilson to read some correspondence which he had had with Mr Monks.

===== 0002601_19030116_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
FATHER WILSON'S CORRESPONDENCE WITH MR. MONKS.

"THE PROPER SPHERE OF THE PRIESTS' ACTION." "MISNAMED INTERFERENCE."

Father WILSON, who had a very hearty reception.said that since his attention was called to the report of the case which was under consideration, he saw at once that It was likely to rouse feeling and to call for somestrong action. Therefore as a preliminarystep he lost no time in sending a report of the case to Mr. Monks and asking him in a single sentence whether he had been truthfully and correctly reported in that paper. He got this reply : "Monks, Hall and Co., Limited, Warrington, Bth January, 1903. Dear Father Wilson,—l have your letter of the 7th, and the report is substantially correct, and it expresses the feeling of the magistrates in reference to the case that came before us oh Monday. We felt that it was most unfortunate the child was removed from the care and custody of one who had given it every care and affection and was very much attached to the child, and handed over to a woman who is utterly incapable of looking after herself properly, much less looking after a child six months old, and in addition five other young children. The woman now declines to have the child back again, with the result that it will be thrown back again into a family evidently doomed to be pauperized instead of being brought up in a respectable family, where it would have had a fair I chance in the future.—Yours faithfully, F. MONKS." _ _ _ _

By the time that letter reached him i,t became quite evident that the remarks of Mr. Monks from the Bench were of such a character as to call for some action and some answer. At that time he had no idea of the depth of feeling that had been aroused. Neither had he any idea what action could or would be taken. After a little thought it seemed to him that it was his duty, as the incident occurred in their parish and concerned one of his zealous assistant priests—(applause)—to do what he could, as he always tried to do, to safeguard their honour as Catholics and their interests. (Hear, hear). Consequently he wrote this answer : "St. Mary's, Warrington, January 9th, 1903. Dear sir,—l beg to acknowledge your letter, dated Bth inst., and also to note with satisfaction the contrast which, in its greater moderation of expression, it forms to your rather heated and very offensive utterances from the Bench. (Hear, hear). -You evidently do not realise how deeply you have wounded the feelings, , not of myself or fellow-priests—of that I say nothing

===== 0002601_19030116_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
had regretted removing the child ever since, it indicates that he had unwillingly submitted to the authority of the Church, but Ido not care to press this point. (Laughter.) As to Nero and Diocletian—(laughter)— they were Roman and not English—(" Very good") and I pass that with the remark that Rome has always been remarkable for 'enforcing them with logical sever- ity and bloody conclusion.' (Laughter.) You have just the same right to your liberty that we have, and I don't know that anyone wishes to curtail it,or even wish for a suppression of your freedom so long as you don't abuse it. (Laughter.) Now, let me say in conclusioh, that I not only expressed my own view,but also the five or six other magistrates that sat on the bench at the same time, and lam authorized to say that if a similar case were to cd-ne before them they would feel it their duty to give even a stronger expression of their dis- approval of such action.—Yours faithfully, F. MONKS."

CON DEMNATO RY RESOLUTION. MR. MA-GINN AND "LANGUAGE OF THE TAPROOM."

Mr. J. MAGINN moved the following resolution : "That Mr. Monks, J.P., the chairman of the Bench of Magistrates upon the hearing of the above case, ex- hibited a lamentable want of magisterial judgment, tact, courtesy and discretion in accepting without investigation the charges made by the prisoner against the Rev. Dr. Mercer, and that in criticizing the Catholic priests and other ministers of religion in Warrington and lecturing them as to their present and future rela- tions with their several comiTegations,he offered them a deliberately, gratuitous and wanton insult, and unfairly travelled beyond the limits and privileges of a magis- trate." (Applause.) Mr. Maginn spoke very warmly I and characterised neglect of children as one of the most heinous crimes of which a father could guilty. He referred in terms of praise to Mrs. Rylatt's action in! taking charge of the child, but considered it unfair that the child should be reared in opposition to the relig- ion of its father, its brothers and sisters, and its dead ' mother. He considered it an act of manliness on Cullen's part to refuse to allow the child to be brought up as a Protestant. If Cullen had done his best with his money he could have kept his children well. The prisoner had said that pressure had been brought to bear upon him to take the child back, and the magistrate jumped up and said "This is a piece of priestly intolerance.," Such language was the language of the taproom. (Cheers.) It *as language which had been used tp them eve since he had been born, and their fathers before them,r by an ignorant bully. They would, however, take the remarks for just what they were worth. Probably they were uttered on the spur of the moment. After the man was sentenced the woman was dis- charged for some reason that he did not know, and Mr. Monks tried to make the man see that he did not give him three months for his crime but just as a warn- ing to him that he would not let the priest interfere again. What was said about the priest was taken as God Almighty's truth. and it seemed to him (the speaker) that Mr. Monks must be a bigot in his heart in taking the word of a criminal,guilty of the most heinous crime —the ill-treatment of children—against the word of a gentlemen, and an educated gentleman too. (Cheers.) Would not a sensible man have said "We will adjourn the case until to-morrow in order to get Dr. Mercer to give evidence"? (Cheers.) They, as Catholics, had felt the criticism of their priest very severely. If the same deliberate and personal insult had been offered to a clergyman of the Church of England—the Catholics were only 10,000 in the town-30,000 people would have been found to protest. And why should not the Catholigs protest? (Applause.) Mr. Monks had had a full wMlcand a day to consider the remarks he had made, and his only consideration was to get up a religious controversy, and God save them from that ! (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. Monks seemed to hold a brief for bigotry, bat he would not be allowed to air himself at the expense of Catholics. (Cheers.) Let it be distinctly understood that they did not want as Catholics to make this a religious controversy or to enlighten the outside public about their belief and faith. Nor did they care about the belief of others. They must not let Mr. Monks coax them to get into trouble with their Protestant friends. Let them not start the argu- ment on religion, because if they did they would play into Mr. Monks, hands. (Cheers.) Their only reason for holding that meeting was to vindicate their cause. They did not dispute the justiceof Mr Monk.s's decision, but they denied his right to try people who were not at the Police Court. (Cheers.) Mr. C. H. UNswoRTH in seconding the resolution, said there was very little that he could add, or desired • to add, to the facts of the case. The correspondence which had been read sufficiently illustrated all the points which could be brought to bear upon the matter. There was, however, one point he should like to put before the meeting, namely, that Mr. Monks seemed to think Father Mercer was doing something he had no right to do in insisting upon the child being brought up a Catholic. It would have been Mr. Monks's duty, no less than Father Mercer's, if one of the Cullen children had been brought before him who was over five years of age—and the fact that it was only 12 months old made I no difference in principle—not to hand the child over to a Protestant institution but to a Catholic industrial school: because it was provided by the State, which was a great deal more liberal in its views to Catholics than Mr. Monks—(applause)—under the Industrial Schools • Act, 1866, that magistrates should send such children to Catholic schools if they happened to be Catholics. Therefore Mr. Monks was reprimanding Father Mercer for something he was bound to do him- self jf the child Irid been brought before him. (Cheers.) The resolution was unanimously carried. APPEAL TO THE CHANCELLOR OF THE DUCHY

===== 0002601_19030116_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
OF LANCASTER.

Mr. ECKERSLEY then moved the following resolution: "That a copy of the proceedings in the Cullen case, as rep orted in the Press, be forwarded to the Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster. together with a copy of the further resolutions passed at the public meeting; that the same be sent to Mr. Monks and the Press." They were not present, ha said, to protest against priestly in tolerance, but against magisterial intolerance. (Cheers.) Mr. RICHARDSON said he would content himself by briefly seconding the resolution. The previous sp3akers had taken the wind out of his sails. The motion was carried with enthusiasm. THE PRIESTS AND THEIR DUTIES.

The Rev. Father WILSON, in proposing a vote of thanks to the Chairman, said the duties of priests were sometimes of a very anxious character, and they had often to do and say things that were necessarily unpleasant, and often to meet attacks and utterances that were not pleasant to put up with, and at Such times to find that the laity were loyal and were ready to sup- port them, was undoubtedly a great help. (Hear, hear.) The largeaudience that evening spoke more eloquently than any set speeches could speak—(cheers)—of the bond between the priests and themselves. Their great hope was,that that bond might never be loosened. (Cheers). An occasional outburst of this kind, and an occasional meeting, and even an oecasional attack upon I them; might be fruitful of good results. (Hear, hear.) These things let them know what they were, and gate them a consciousness of being a body corporate scattered about a large town. They were apt sometimes to lose touch with one another. An occasion like th is brought them to,,bether,and if they would stick together —it was only when side issues and minor considerations were allowed to creep in and divide them that they need be fearful—they never need be fearful of having to take a back seat or give way to those who were hostile to them on account of their religion. (Cheers). The Rev. Father WHITTLE seconded, and remarked that no doubt good would come out of evil. An insult had been thrown against one of their priests, but the audio ace by their presence that night had shown they would not stand it. (Cheers.) He quite agreed with Father Wilson that this meeting would bring them closer together, if that were possible—cement closer the union between people and priest. (Cheers.) They were met as Catholics, and before they left that build- ing they would produce a sound in it that had never been heard before. They would sing "Faith of our fathers." (Cheers.) The Rev. Father MCGARTH, of St. Mary's, Latchford, in supporting the resolution, said that for over nine years he worked in the most bigoted town, he supposed, in the North of England, but he never heard, let alone from a magisterial Bench, such words as those that had been uttered against Catholics in Warrington recently. He wished to enter his protest against the words that had been used by Mr. Monks against Roman Catholics. They were a slap in the face for every priest and every slap in the face for the priest was a slap in the face for the laity. (Hear. hear.) They would not stand it, and they would concentrate and rally round each other and let it be known what their strength was. They would show that they would not take these instills wantonly, sad that they were not doormats any moie than anyone else. As long as they had a tongue to wag they would wag it—(cheers)—and make themselves felt if they were insulted to their teeth. (Renewed cheers.) The resolution was then put to the meeting and unanimously carried. The Chairman briefly acknowledged the compliment and the audience then stood and sang with great fervour "Faith of our Fathers."

Lord aud Lady Cromer and Sir Reginald and Lady Wingate passed Meshra-el-Rhom, the last telegraphic station on the White Nile and 400 miles south of Khar- toum, on Monday evening, on their way southwards It is expected that telegraphic communication with Pashoda will very shortly be established. A great automobile tramway has been started at Lisbon, with a capital of 600,000,000 reis, to fight the existing English Electric Tramway Company. The news has caused a great sensation. At Whitchurch, near Aylesbury, on Wednesday after- noon, a lad named Elijah John Batson,aged ten, was killed accidently by his elder brother. THE PROPOSED TRAMWAY TO STOCKTON HEATH. The Parish Counci D3Cit ta Support

the Scheme.

===== 0002601_19030116_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
Agreement with the Corporation.

A special meeting of the Stockton Heath Parish Council was held on Tuesday evening td consider the Warrington Corporation Light Railway Order under which the Corporatidn propose to extend their tramway system from Stafford-road to Victoria-square, Stockton Heath. Mr. J. H. Clarke presided and there were also present: Messrs. Pemberton,Bouchier, Pearson, Mclntyre and Fairhurst, with Mr. Bradshaw (clerk). The Chairman observed that before entering upon the business of the meeting he wished to refer to an extraordinary statement made by Alderman Bolton at the last meeting of the Warrington Town Council. viz. that the Stockton Heath Parish Council did not know what they wanted, and that if they would only meet the Warrington Corporation half-way they would be able to get on very well with the Tramway Order. He (Mr. Clarke) certainly thought that it was an extraor- dinary statement for Alderman Bolton to make. On instituting inquiries he found that there were two excuses for the alderman. One was that he was a new member of the committee, and he (the Chairman) wat to Id that Alderman Bolton did not know what he was talking about—(laughter) ; and the other excuse was that it was always as well never to take what Alderman Bolton said in a serious manner. (Hear, hear, and laugh- ter). As regards the statement about meeting the War- rington Corporation halfway, that Council had not only done so,

A Voice, "three-fourths." (Hear,- hear.) The Chairman, yes, three-fourths, certainly. They (the Stockton Heath Council) had done everything that was reasonable while doing their duty to the inhabitants of StocktonlHeath. (Hear, hear.)

LNTERVIEW WITH THE CLERK OF THE COUNTY COUNCIL.

The Chairman proceeded to give a report on the in- terview which took place between the Clerk and him- self and Mr. Potts (Clerk of the County Council) at Crewe. They explained to Mr. Potts their views. At that time Mr. Potts said he had not considered the new Order very deeply, but he said with reference to the new clause put in, and the plan sent on their behalf by Mr. Bradshaw. the County Council were surprised at seeing it in thZ Order. The opinion he (the Chairman) and Mr. Bradshaw formed,was that the County Council were very much displeased with it, because they (the Parish Council) had made arrangement themselves with the Warrington Town Council. The deputation pointed out that although it was an alteration to what had been agreed upon it was a great improvement upon what had been deciddd upon,between themselves and the Commis- sioners. They pointed out that their clause only gave the Stockton Heath Council 8,451 t. roadway for most of the distance, but the amended plan would make it 50ft. for a great part of the distance ; and the only differance would be a portion in front of the church where they had decided to accept the position the War- rington Corporation were placed in—that they could not go beyond the walls of the building. According to the clause of the County Council they wanted to force the hands of the Corporation to take down the buildings opposite the church. He (the chairman) pointed out that if the hands of the Warrington Corporation were forced to that extent, the Corporation would not go on with their order. They in Stockton Heath could not take upon themselves the responsibility of supporting anything of the:kind. Therefore they wanted the County Council to reconsider it and not to oppose the clause agreed to between the Corporation and the Parish Council. The other point was as to the footpaths. There was the a question of the footpath which was shown on the plan as only being 2ft. 6in wide in certain por- tions. The County Council at once said they would not accept and he (the chairman) asked Mr. Potts to name the width of the footpath they were going to press for and he would ask for the same width. The reply was that he should ask for a sft. footpath. That they accepted and they sent in their request for the sft. footpath to the Board of Trade.

===== 0002601_19030116_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
LETTER FROM THE BOARD OF TRADE

The, Clerk read the following letter from the Board of Trader:—" With reference to the objections lodged by you to the confirmation of the Warrington Corporation Light Railway Order, I am directed by the Board ,of Trade to inform you that they propose to take the Order into consideration at this office (Railway Department, 7, Whitehall Gardens), on Tuesday, January 27th, at 12 o'clock, when an opportunity will be given to objectors or their representatives of being heard in support of their objections."

THE POSITION OF THE CORPORATION. LETTER FROM THE TOWN CLERK.

The Chairman informed the meeting that Mr. Whittle, the Town Clerk of Warrington, had told him bsi tele- phone that he had received the objections of the tounty Council, and that he had been instructed by his Committee to let the Stockton Heath Council know that if the objections were sustained by the Board of Trade and entered in the Order,the Corporation would not go any further with the scheme. He (the Chairman) told him that the Parish Council did not agree with the County Council persisting in anything that was likely to stop the Order. He asked Mr. Whittle whether he had any objection to what the Parish Council proposed, and he said he had not. He (Mr. Clarke) said that if the Council could help the Corporation by writing to the Board of Trade or the County Council they would be willing to do so. Mr. Bradshaw read the following letter from the Town Clerk :—" Referring to your letter of the 16th ult. I am directed to inform you that the CorpGration will offer no objection to the proposed alterations of the footpaths by reducing the width of the carriageway. With regard to the question as to whether there shall be la single or double line of rails in front of St. Thomas' Church, the Corporation are prepared to leave it to the ; Board of Trade. As you are aware, the County Council are asking the Board of Tradeto require the Corporation ; to widen Old London-road beyond the limits agreed to with your Council. If the Board of Trade impose this I condition on the Corporation I am instructed to inform ' the Board that the Corporation will not proceed with ' the Order. You will remember that the County Council asked us to,give them a road of 45ft. in width. We have ; agreed to widen the road for two-thirds of its length to ; 50ft., and at the end, near the Mulberry Tree, it increases to 55ft. The line of the proposed widening at this end is shown on the deposited plan as running through premises other than those which the Corporation pro- pose to purchase. and the County Council are pressing for additional widening through those premises. They are also asking "that the verandah in front of the premises near the swing bridge shall not only be ac- -1 saired but removed." If these matters are persisted in, it may mean that you will lose the Order altogether. I would, therefore, suggest that you make a strong repre- sentation to the Clerk of the County Council in refer _ ; ence to them. lam informed by the Board of Trade ; that the Runcorn Rural District Council have also raised objections to the Order. I do not know what I points they have raised as they have not sent me a copy ;of their objections. I think the Corporation are being put to unnecessary expense in having an inquiry forced upon them. As requested, I enclose copy of the amend- ments proposed by the County Council." I The Chairman: If there is the slightest danger of I the tramways not going on,we must leave no stone Un- turned, but do everything that is reasonable and can be expected from us. ,• Mr. Bouchier : I suggest that the clerk should write I to the County Council asking them to withdraw, as far as this matter is concerned, their opposition to the Bill. The Chairman: Yes. I take it that we represent the feeling of the people of Stockton Heath by saying that we are prepared to accept the clause as amended by the Commissioners subject to our own proposition being agreed to as stated in the Town Clerk's letter. I and take it that we now become supporters of the cheme. (Several members. "Yes."). - Mr. Bouchier : I move that the Clerk be instructed , to write in these terms to the Clerk of the County I Council. I Mr. Pearson seconded. The Chairman: That if the Warrington Corporation I accepted our proposals we support them. Mr. Pearson: And give them all the assistance we can.

Mr. Pemberton: Have the Corporation embodied what you want in the Order? The Chairman: They say they won't offer any opposition. Mr. Bouchier : L don't think it is necessary for any- one representing the Parish Council to Appear at the inquiry, if we write to the Board of Trade, stating that the Corporation have agreed to accept our proposals. Mr. Pearson! It would be a calamity to the village if the tramway scheme was dropped by Warrington. I would not be our fault. We have been very reasonable,t and we have never deviated from a certain line which we always considered reasonable. I should like to sug- gest that the Clerk be sent to London to support the Warring ton Corporation. The Chairman : _ If desired.

Pearson: Yes, if desired. It was then decided that this Council should be repre- sented at the inquiry,if the Board of Trade considered

t necessary.

Institute of Journalists —Warrington Sub-

District.

The annual meeting of the members of the War- rington sub-district of the Liverpool district of the Institute o Journalists was held on Saturday night in a room at the George Hotel, kindly lent by Mr. Halton. Mr. T. Weston presided over a good attend- ance. A vote of condolence with the wife and family of the late Mr. Phillip Smith, of Liverpool, was passed in silence. Mr. E. V. Walker was elected chairman for the ensuing year, and the retiring chairman (Mr. T. Weston) vice-chairman, the latter being thanked for his services during the past year. Owing to his removal to Runcorn, Mr. F. Fennelly resigned his position as hon. secretary and treasurer, arid Mr. A. H. Walker was appointed to fill the vacancy. Mr. Fennelly was cordially thanked for the admirable way in which he had discharged the duties for ti e past two years. Several matters of interest to local journalists were discussed, and the meeting closed with votes of thanks to the Chairman and to Mr. Halton for the use of the room.

FAINTING FITS.

! It is surprising what a number of people feel suddenly dizzy and faint. Such people feel afraid to ascend I stairs, to cross crowded streets or to go to public; gatherings. Like most distressing weaknesses, fainting and dizzi- ness has a cause. One of many reasons is shown by Mr. Richard Wilson, of Stamford Bridge, Yorkshire, in his letter of March sth, 1902. The opening sentences of his letter tell the whole story. "I have very great pleasure," he says," in recommend' ing "Dodd's Kidney Pills, which are a grand remedy for giddiness, pains in the back, and all kidney troubles.' Mr. Wilson suffered from these complaints for si%- years, and it was not until he began to take Dodd's Kidney Pills that he overcame his weakness. Giddiness, dizziness, and faintness are symptoms of kidney disorder caused chiefly by the irregularity of the circulation, the blood being a stream of poisons which the kidney's ought to take out of the system. The cure is a pure blood supply, and this is only gained by making the i kidneys act properly. There s nothing better in the world than Dodd's Kidney Pills for ailing kidneys. Dodd's Kidney Pills are 2/9 per box' of all eheinist,' or post free on receipt of price from The Dodds Wu' cine Co., 23, Farringdon Avenue. London, E.C. Be sure of the name, D-o-d-d-'s.

GOOD TEMPLAR CONVENTION AT WARRINGTON.

On Saturday afternoon, the members of the No. 1 Convention, S.W. Lancashire met for their monthly session in the Ebenezer Church, Buttermarket-street. Bro. J. Parr, C.C.T., presided at the opening,when there Fasa goodattendance of representatives from Warring- ton, Earlestown, St. Helens, Widnes, Wigan, Leigh and .Ashton. Bro. R. D. Petterson,(Dt. Marshall) was invited to take the chair after the opening ceremony. After the usual business, the visiting officers presented their reports as follows :—Bro. Parr, C.C.T. (Leigh) reported i.anthel-lindley lodge; Bro. G. Clarke, C.
