The Examiner, Friday, January 16, 1903.

===== 0002601_19030116_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
ON LOCAL CHURCHES.

IVyclifie P.S.A. Society. The 'fleeting of this society was presided over by Mr. T. L. Robins, who spoke on Gods watchfulness, thought- fulness and care over His people. No matter what trouble, loneliness or difficulties befel us, God was always the same re'ady to help and cheer. Mr. C. Fooks, of Manchester, was the speaker, and he delivered an address on " Judas and Peter.' Both of these men, he pointed out, were guilty men, but one was pardoned and the other not, because Judas sought not forgiveness by true repentance. When Judas thought of Christ in all that he had seam Him in, full of goodness and love to all around, he could not look upon Christ again, but destroyed himself. If he had truly turned to Christ he would have found mercy and forgiveness. The attitude of Judas was compared with that of Peter after his denial of Christ. Christ loved and forgave Peter because he was ‘, >rry for wrong doing. The speaker proceeded o hold up Peter's attitude as an example to be followed.—The service was also taken part in by Mr. G. Smiley, Mr. D. Owen and Mr. Farrar (hon. secretary). Holy Trinity Church.

There were good congregations at this church on Sun- day when the annual choir festival was held. The Vicar preached at the morning service. A voluntary "As pants the hart," with variations was skilfully played by the organist, Mr. Percy Randall: Miss P. Houghton, of Daresbury, gave a feeling interpretation of the song "0 Divine Redeemer," and Miss Alice Longdin effectively sang Liddle's "Abide with me." Miss M. Leigh and Mr. F. H. Randall gave a creditable rendering of Handel's "Largo" on their violins, while Mr. T. D. Amos sang the sacred song "Nazareth" (Gounod) in good style. The choir ably rendered the anthem "Break forth into joy." The Magnific,at and Num: Dimittis were sung to settings by Maunder in G and it may be stated that the Cathedral Psalter is in future to be used in the church. The Vicar delivered an address, and spoke in high terms of the efficiency of the choir, and explained that the collections would be devoted to the purchase of new music. In the even- ing the preacher was the Rev. A. Inglis. The collec- tions for the day amounted to about £lO.

===== 0002601_19030116_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
The Free Churches.

The Evangelical Free Churches of Warrington held their annual exchange of pulpits and united communion service on Sunday evening. The exchange of pulpits was greatly appreciated by the various congregations, and the large attendance at the united communion service was an evidence of the unity of spirit amongst, the Evangelical Free Churches of the town. At Wycliffe Congregational Church, the preacher was the Rev. Her- bert Dunnico ; at Emmanuel Reformed Church, Rev. G. H. Carnson; at Bold-street Wesleyan Church, Rev. J. L. Milne, B.D. ; at Bewsey-street Wesleyan Church, Rev. J. F. Matthews; at Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Church, Rev. J. G. Reed; at Golborne-street Baptist Church, Rev. A. G. D. Gee; at Latchford Baptist Church, Rev. W. C. J. Neville ; at St. John's Presbyterian Church,Rev. J. Yonge: at Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church, Rev. R. W. Rees. The united communion service was held shortly ' after the termination of the usual services. The Rev. John Yonge presided,and extended a cordial welcome to all present. He trusted that as the result of the prayer meetings during tho past week and the united corn-' mnnion service- that day, they would have a new baptism of the holy ghost. For this let them pray and work. The Rev. G. H. Carnson, the superintendent Wesleyan minister, delivered an address and pointed out that they were met that night as a community of Christian churches. For the nonce they sank their differences of opinion on matters of Christian doctrine and were one in Christ. He proceeded to speak on the meaning of the communion service and of the import- ance of the pledge which the sacrament conveyed.

===== 0002601_19030116_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
St. Paul's Church.

The offertories at - this Church on Sunday were on behalf of the Church of England Incorporated Society for providing homes for waifs and strays, and the ser- vices were well-attended. The Vicar (Rev. W. Brace- camp) was the preacher in the morning, and the Rev. E. Tarbuck, Vicar of St. George's Altrincham, and formerly Vicar of St. Paul's, occupied the pulpit in the evening. The rev gentlemen emphasised the good work done by the Society and appealed to the congregations for liberal contributions. What is called a "pound" service was held in the afternoon. The members of the congregation each brought a pound of tea, rice,flouroSce., wrapped in white paper and these were placed in front of the altar and afterwards taken to one of the homes for use by the waifs and strays. In regard to the work of the Society it is intereting to note that the Liverpool Diocesan Branch has four homes and over 180 children, with one other at Southport for cripples, maintained by the Northern Children's Union. The Society's objects are to rescue, maintain, and start in life, children who have been left destitute, or whose parents or relations prove, after the fullest investiga- tion,unable to provide for the n. The houses are not large institutions—with few exceptions 30 children constitute the limit of one home—and are for boys learning carpen- try, printing, farming, or gardening • some for girls training for domestic service, laundry-work, needle- work, stocking or slipper-making. Very young children Are boarded out with carefully chosen foster parents. Three receiving homes in Canada provide security for emigrated cases. The income from this diocese is now about £2,Boo—The offertories for the day realized .Cl 5 ss. `2.d.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

FOR FANCY ARTICLES Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday. Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. We cannot under ordinary circumstances undertake to return the manuscripts which are not used.

The Council and the Inquiry.

Sir.—May I be allowed to make through your columns an earnest appeal, to the Mayor, Aldermen and Coun- cillors of the County Borough of Warringtonto re-con- sider their action at the last Council meeting. _

In view of the probable consequences, an in the in- terest of peace and quietness may I venture to suggest that it might be the most advisable fo; all parties, for the proposition of the non-traders' to be allowed to stand as suggested by the resolution passed by the Council, but as far as I can gather, rescinded at the last meeting. The representatives of the non-traders in the Council have received their mandate direct from the ratepayers and if their wishes be utterly disregarded, the effect must be, that the agitation will break out in a more violent form.

This is the only natural conclusion to arrive at; for the evident fear manifested by the Council in seeking to limit the inquiry will increase the feeling of suspicion which unfortunately has been created by the desire in the past to keep back the information thepablic desired to have. _ _ _

If there is nothing wrong or improper why shouldany investigation be dreaded and if everything proves hon- est anesatisfactory it is much better that the Council and the public should be thus assured, at the earliest possible moment. As a Ratepayer who signed the requisition to our present Mayor to honour the town by accepting the position, I hope he will quite understand that none of us have the slightestidea of questioning his impartiality and good judgment, hilt the fear is, he has not taken the course adopted on his own initiative, but probably has been officially advised and has littlepersonal feeling one way or the other. Personally, I, along with many townsmen are parti- cularly gratified by his acceptance of the Mayoralty and we trust he will not entertain the idea of resigning, through this little friction, but will continue to hold office and see the thing through to the satisfaction of the ratepayers. Whether the latter are right or wrong in their modest request it is not for me to determine, but I hope the members of the Council who differ from them,will in the interests of good Government, peace,and good-will, accede to what they demand, and set at rest this feeling of uncertainty now existing,—faithfully yours, R. A. NAYLOR. West End Hotel,Pwllheli, January 14th, 1903.

Our readers will recollect that at the Council meet- ing Mr. Naylor refers to, Mr. Dodds' resolution was not rescinded, as the motion in the name of Alderman Monks was not voted upon.—EDIToR, "Examiner."

Enquirer's Reply' to Mr. R. A. Naylor.

Sir,—From the tone of Mr. Naylor's last letter it is abundantly evident that I have demolished his position. He began this discussion by accusing somebody whom he either cannot or will not name, of "picking the pockets of the ratepayers." When taken to task for his unjust accusations, in a short and capable letter by Mr. Monks, Mr. Naylor rejoined by charging this gentleman with "personal abuse" and "scurrilous language." To myself, Mr. Naylor cannot find words sufficiently vituperative to apply, simply because he is unable to refute my statements and, in lieu of argument, gives in his letters a most apt illustration of the old Attorney's advice to his junior" when you have no case, abuse the other side." Mr. Naylor lays very great stress on the fact of my " anonymity " but, if my statements are true and my deductions logical, how is Mr. Naylor's position adversely affected simply because I choose to conceal my name? Does Mr. Naylor forget or is he so ignorant as not to know, that the most powerful factor on the side of the Non-Traders has been, not the Parr Hall meetings,but the recognised organ of;the Committee —the " Warrington Times," in which, week after week, written under no signature, have appeared insolent, abusive and false statements which, by the gratuitous circulation of the paper have created that atmosphere of distrust of neariy all our public men which has been so despicable a feature of the agitation. Does Mr. Naylor justify the men under whose auspices this kind of "hitting below the belt " has been carried on. Is Mr. Naylor playing the man" in allowing himself to be dragged into the forefront of a "cause" which has to descend so low to secure its objects? Has Mr. Naylor, in all the years that he has been occupied in business in Warrington ever shown himself in the slightest degree interested in our civic affairs until coincident with the defeat of his firm in competition for a tender for wood blocks he appeared on the plat- form of the Non-Traders? I leave him to answer these pertinent questions and pass on again for a moment to his attitude in regard to the alleged nuisance at Thel- wall. In this matter Mr. Naylor has spared nobody who ventured to differ from him—he has written letters by the score, grammatical and otherwise, roundly abusing the local authority differing with the officials, pitching into the supposed culprits, deploring the havoc wrought by disease and death,thought by him to be resultant from the "nuis- ance "and yet, it has never occurred to him, rich man as he is either to go to law with the delinquents or better to put his hand in his pocket as the "charit- able benefactor " of the district and put a stop to all the mischief by carrying out what he believes to be the necessary sanitary reforms at his own cost. It is a very easy matter for Mr. Naylor to write letters from London and Pwllheli denouncing the evil which is supposed to exist at Thelwall, but such letters don't afford much help to the poor folks whose want of means makes it impossible for them to leave the district,and I put it to Mr. Naylor that there are other forms of "cowardice" which some rich men indulge in compared with which the writer, who states facts under an anoymous signa- ture,has naught to be ashamed of. Yours Brc. EN QUIRE R.

===== 0002601_19030116_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LONDON POISONING CASE. More Evidence Against Chapman.

The charges of poisoning three women—Maud Eliza Marsh, in October, 1902, Bessie Taylor. in February, 1901, and Mary Isabella Spink, in August, 1837—pre- ferred against Severino Kolsowsky, otherwise George Chapman, late landlord of the Crown Inn, Borough, were further investigated on Tuesday, before Mr. Cecil Chapman, at the Southwark Police Court, London. Several witnesses gave evilence as to the prisoner's life at Hastings. One stated that Chapman and the woman who passed as his wife quarrelled, and the woman afterwards was taken ill. A female said she lent the prisoner four volumes of the "Family Physician." Alice Pensold said that about six years ago she was in the employment of Mrs. Field at Hastings. She became acquainted with Chapman and walked out with him about three times. Once she told the prisoner she had a cold, and he said he would give her something to do her cold good, adding that he had been in a hospital. He sent her a note and some powders. She did not take the powders; she threw them on the fire. He asked her if she lad taken them, and she answered "No." The prisoner made no reply to that. Once when he wrote from Hastings he signed himself George Smith, but when writing from London he signedhimself George Chapman. The London letter bore the address "Prince of Wales, St. Luke's," and asked witness to come to London and see him. Witness did not accept the invitation, and she had not heard from Lhe prisoner since. The prisoner was again remanded.

FUNERAL_ OF MRS. SMITH, NEWTON.. •

The funeral of Mrs. James Smith;of Brookside, New: ton, whose death we chronicled last week, took place an Saturday at Ince Cemetery. Previous to the cortege leaving Newton, d short service was held in St. Peter's Church, the Rev. James Ryder officiating. Amongst those present in the Church were the Rev. J. Carson, Messrs. R. Stone, C. B. F. Borron, R. Barton, &c. The cortege afterwards proceeded by road •to Ice. The chief mourners were Mr. Arthur Smith, Mr. W. H. Smith, Mr. J. P. D. Smith, and Mr. Sydney Smith (sons), Mrs. Bradley (Manchester), Mrs. Watkins and Mrs. Woods, Wigan (daughters). There were also present : Drs. Bradley. and H. Watkins (sons-in-law), Mrs. A. Smith, Mrs. J. Smith (Wigan), Mr. G. Clarke, Miss Pennington, Mr. G. Airey, Mr. R. Airey, Miss Airey, Mr. and Mrs. C. B. Watkins and Dr. Watkins. There were a large number of wreaths from relatives and friends.

The Examiner, Friday, Januacy 16, 1903.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL Rugby Football. Dr. Latham and Lord Newton's Gift.

By " Ovalist."

The unexpected did not happen on Saturday, and the weak team Warrington sent to Brighouse was defeated by 7 points to nil. This is not a severe beating, but as the Rangers had only won three matches previ- ously, it makes Warrington appear rather "small beer." The cloud has still to lift and show the -silver lining. Just now the Warrington club's supporters are despondent.

111-hick again attended the Warrington team on Saturday, for the loss of the toss had a great deal to do with the result of the game. During the first half Warrington had to face a regular blizzard of snow and rain as well as play uphill, and it was while they laboured under these disadvantages that Brighouse scored their seven point'). In the second half War- rington had the better of the play but were unable to score, though Hockenhall had a try disallowed.

The two "A " teamers, Bailey and Hunter, both acquitted themselves very well and gave such satis- faction that they are selected for the St. Helens match which comes off to-day, weather and other circum- stances permitting. Hallam shone again in defensive work and saved Knight frequently when the latter was none too steady: The forwards also did well and got the better of the Brighouse eight in the later stages of the game.

Warrington " A " disported themselves at home against Widnes White Star, whom they defeated by one try, scored by Watkins, to nil. The play was often ragged but generally interesting, Warrington's weakest place being half-back. 'Here Webb and Brough officiated, the former being the bettar of the two. He did some smart things at times, but needs practice. The three-quarter line and full back (Farrimond) did well, Burgess and Watkins both being very energetic on the wing. The Stars played a resolute game, but were not quite fast enough to da any scoring. •

For the St. Helens match the team has undergone further changes. Hallam returns to full back,l3urgess plays centre, and Bailey takes the place of Harris, while Dickens,m and Fish are on the other wing. The full side will be: Hallam, full back; Fish, Dicken- son, Burgess and Bailey, threequarters •, Hockenhall and Hunts-, half-hacks; Boardman, Morrison, W. Swift, F. Swift, Edmondson, Naylor, Roberts, and Harmer forwards. Isherwood is not yet fit.

This team ought to be good enough to defeat the Saints, who succumbed to Warrington on their own ground early in the season by 18 points to five. They have been vowing vengeance ever since, however, and mean to make Warrington go all the way this time.

Dickenson and Fish both played well for Lancashire against Cumberland at Millom on Saturday, and the latter scored the try of the afternoon, after a run the length of the field. Lancashire won by 3 goals 5 tries to one try.

Fish's amateur status on the running path came up again at the N.C.A.A. on Wednesday. The decision of the committee has been pending during the last two months, and it was resolved that Fish be not eligible to compete as an amateur. This means that " Jack" will have to cease running in the summer or join the ranks of the professionals.

The Warrington officials are making a big effort to get some new blood ere the cup-tie registration papers are sent in. Recent results have proved the want of it more conclusively than ever, and it is i perative that there should be some "importing." Other clubs are managing to pick up men here and there, and there is not much excuse for the failures of the Warrington emissaries. The Bradford club have secured the services of G. H. Lewis, forward,age 26, height-sft. 8-}in„ and -weight 13st. 41b., a Welsh- min who has played with West Hartlepool and Dur- ham County. Another capture by the same club is James Dechan (23). a wing three-quarter back, of Hayrick.

Swinton have fared so poorly when R. Valentine is away, that a special train will be employed on Satur- day to take him from Rochdale to Brighouse. He will travel ordinary to Rochdale. This arrangement has become necessary on account of the fact that his employers will not let him leave business before the other employes.

The County calls played havoc in Broughton and Salford,as well as Warrington. Both teams were badly beaten, the former at Hull and the latter at home by Wigan. Runcorn accomplished a fine performance against Bradford whom they beat 15-9 after being 7 points behind at half time.

Mr. T. Ormand presided at the monthly meeting of the Newton Urban District Council which was held at Earlestown Town Hall on Monday evening. There were also present :—Messrs. C. B. F. Borron, J. W. Emmett, N. Siddeley, W. Gilmour. S. J. Wood, J. Simpson, E. T. Evans, and Dr. Latham, with C. Cole (clerk), R. T Surtees (gas and water engineer), and T. Bowes (surveyor). THE CHAIRMAN'S GOOD WISHES.

Cycling.

Very many of the Warrington cyclists have at one time or another stayed at the Eivion Hotel,Llangollen, and it will he a source of regret to them to learn that the popular proprietor, Mr. Clarke, lost his youngest daughter, Ethel, on the Sunday following Christmas day. The deceased girl was only nineteen. Christ- mas week has, in the past, been a sad time for Host Clarke. and I am sure the sympathies of the Warring- ton wheelmen will go out to him in this fresh bereave- ment.

===== 0002601_19030116_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
ANGLING.

The Committee of the Warrington Anglers' Associa- tion recently decided to accept theoffer of Mr. T. Ford, Caistor, Lincolnshire, to supply 1,000 roach, 4 to 9ins.long for stocking purposes in the Old Quay Canal, Walton. The arrangements were pushed for- ward, and on Saturday afternoon last, the fish arrived at Central Station. An efficient staff under the superintendence of Mr. P. Knight and a conveyance were in attendanCe. On a close inspection ot the fish being made immediately prior to being turned into tho Canal, it was found that many exceeded the size guaranteed by the vendor, and the party having charge of the arrangements were highly pleased with the consignineut as a whole. The greatest credit is due to Mr. Ford for the expeditious manner in which the fish were forwarded, and for the quility of the consignment, as many fish were quite three-quarters of a pouud each. Not a single fish was lost in transit.

The annual supper in connection with the Traveller's Rest Society was held on Monday, when a fair num- ber of members sat down to a capital repast, which had been provided by Mr. awl Mrs. J. Wrench. After supper, the distribution of prizes took place, the winners being T. Worrall, J. Crossley, T. Leigh, and W. Leicester. The rest of the evening was spent in a convivial matter, songs being rendered by various meMbers and friends.

THE NEW LICENSING ACT.

"NEARLY QUALIFIED."

Samuel Sixstnith, Orford-lane, was charged at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday with having been drunk and incapable in Allen-street, on Monday' night. P.C. Clough proved the case,. The magistrates fined the prisoner ss. and costs, the Chief Constable remarking that he was "nearly qualified" for the "black list,". having been before the court six times in a brief period.

THE MAYOR AND THE ACT. "EXTREMELY DRASTIC."

The Mayor (Mr. J. C. Parr) commented on the new Licensing Act at the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday. He said: Before we begin our business at the first session after the passing of the new Licensing Act, I feel very strongly that the Act is throwing an immense responsibility—a very grave responsibility— not only on the Bench, but indirectly also on the police. The Act is extremely drastic and may in cases if not very carefully and very cautiously administered, lead to very great hardships and I was going to say almost cruelty. But I have such confidence in the Bench and in the care of the cases that will be brought before the Bench,that I think nothing of the'sort will occur, at all events in our district.

THE MAGISTERIAL DIFFICULTY.

Dr. Latham said that some time ago a petition was presented to the Chancellor of the Duchy with reference to the magisterial difficulty in the district. If no reply had been received, i would be advisable for the Clerk to write again pointing out that the difficulty still existed. He understood that a number of cases that ought to have been brought before the local Bench had to be taken to Warrington because magistrates could not be obtained.

The Clerk said he had received no reply from th 3 Chancellor.

Dr. Latham moved that the Clerk again write draw- ing attention to the matter. The Chairman pointed out that the business of the Council was over,and that the matter should be intro- duced in committee. The Council then rose.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

TRAP ACCIDENT AT EARLESTOWN,

On Wednesday a trap containing two persons was round- ing the corner out of Crow-lane into Market-street, at a good speed, when it mounted the footpath and over turned, the occupants being thrown violently out. The trap was damaged but the people were not injured.

THE PENRIT VN CHOIR AT EART,ESTOWN. On Wednesday evening the famous Penrhyn Mitle Choir visited the • Town Hall, .Earlestown and had a good reception from a large audience, chiefly'cornposed of Welsh •people. Selections were made from their lengthy programme, and each item was excellently renderedand loudly applauded. The proceeds were in aid of the locked out quarrymen in Bethesda.

P.S.A.

The usual weekly meeting of the P.S.A. was held in the Co-operative Hall, Earlestown on Sunday. Mr. T. Parr presided over a largo attendance. An interesting address was given by Mr. T. R. Bradley. Miss M. Barr rendered two songs "The Good Shepherd" and the "Bettor Land" in a capable manner. Miss liing recited Tennyson's " Legend Beautiful" in.good'style. Mr. W. Willis accompanied at the piano and Mr. B. Smith pre- sided at the organ.

THE RESULT. OF A FALL.

On Saturday a man named Barton employed as a boiler maker at the Viaduct Works, fell on an iron plate and injured his knee. He WaS attended by Dr. Sykes locum teneas.for Dr. Handyside, but on Monday blood poisoning and inflammation set in, and the case became so serious that it was thought advisable to remove him to the Liverpool Infirmary and he was conveyed there by the 5-40 train on Monday night,the horse ambulance meeting him at the station.

ABSENTEES.

MR. GILMOUR AND THE QUALITY OF THE GAS. The Chairmin apologised for the unavoidable absence of Mr. W. Collingwood, Chairman of the Council, and said Mr. Thompson, Chairman of the Gas Committee, had also written regretting his inability to attend. Mr. Gilmour expressed regret at Mr. Thompson's absence. He remarked that the lighting of the Council Chamber was very bad and if Mr. Thompson had been present he would have been able to have seen for him- self the poor quality of the gas. He (Mr. Gilmour) also intended to ask him some questions. If the light- ing of the Council Chamber was bad, what must the conditions be in the cottages? The Chairman said it would be better if Mr. Gilmour mentioned the matter in committee when Mr. Thomp- son was present and then he could "go " for him personally. The subject then dropped. THE LATE MRS. JAMES SMITH. The Chairman said that since the last meeting they had heard with much regret of the death of Mrs. Smith of Brookside. No doubt all themembers would remem- ber that Mrs. Smith was the widow of the late Mr. James Smith, who was a valued member of the old Board of Commissioners as well as of that Council from February, 1894 to the time of his death in August 1896. He (the speaker) was sure the Council would I agree with him that they ought to express their deep I sympathy and condolence with Councillor Arthur Smith land the other members of the family in their sorrow I and bereavement. _ _ .

The proposition was carried in silence. DR. LATHAM AND LORD NEWTON'S GIFT.

Upon the minutes of the last ordinary meeting com- ing up for confirmation, Dr. Latham drew attention to the following minute:—" Resolved unanimously, that the thanks of the Council be given to Lord Newton for thevery generous gift of a piece of land at the Common, to be used for the purpose of a Recreation Ground, and that his Lordship be informed that the Council feel that he has met them ma very amicable way, and they hope that these relations will continue in the interests of the well-being and improvement of the district." He said that some portions of the minute were accurate but others were not. The minute was accurate op to the words "Recreation ground," but the remainder was inaccurate. The wording of the resolution, to his mind, inferred that the relations had not been of such a char- acter in the past as to conduce to the well-being and improvement of the district. He could only say that the relations which had existed between those in auth- ority and the Council depended not so much upon the members of the Council,but upon the action of the lord of the manor and his subordinates. He also took excep- tion to the portion of the resolution which stated that his lordship be informed that the Council felt he had met them in a very amicable way. That was perfectly inaccurate: It was never stated at the Council meeting that Lord Newton had met the Council in a manner which was perfectly amicable. He (Dr. Latham) was glad to say that he was not alone in the Council in thinking that his lordship had not met the Council in that amicable spirit which the resolution intimated. it was contended, and possibly with some accuracy,. that the Council had no legal proof that the land which had been in dispute belonged to the people.— • Mr. Gilmour asked the chairman if Dr. Latham was not out of order. If he (Mr. Gilmour) had spoken like that on a resolution he should have been called to order and told that he ought to give notice of motion. As seconder of the resolution, he contended that Dr. Latham's observations were not true. Dr. Latham was present when the resolution was passed and it was then that he should have spoken. The Chairman (to Dr. Latham) : You are only en- titled to question the accuracy of the minutes and not to make a speech in doing so. If you will tell the members of the Council which words are wrong and which are right, what ought to be deleted and what ought to be left in, I will put it to the meeting and it is for the Council to say whether it is right or wrong; but I must rule you out of order in making a speech upon the matter. Dr. Latham: I was very shortly to come to that. The Chairman: You must excuse me' but I cannot allow this speech to go on. You have been speaking now for seven minutes on the resolution in pointing out an inaccuracy which I have failed to see. Dr- Latham said that if the minutes were confirmed he would have to give notice of motion to rescind the resolution in question. It was not strictly accurate to say that the motion was unanimously passed because no voices were raised' against it. All voices were not raised in its favour. - -

The Chairman, as mover of the resolution, said that to his mind the minute was in spirit and in letter a per- fect and correct interpretation of what he said at the meeting. He failed to see that the resolution implied that the relations between Lod Newton and the Coun- cil had not been happy in the past. The Council sin- cerely hoped that those happy relations which had existed between Lord Newton and the Council on all occasions would continue in the interests of the well- being and improvement of the district. Mr. Borron considered that the minute represented the spirit in which the Council passed the resolution. He was surprised that a member who was present at the meeting should raise the point again that day. It was on Dr. Latham's own showing merely a technical point, and he (Mr. Borron) thought it was entirely out of taste and not in harmony with the custom of the Council.

The minutes were then put to the vote and con- firmed.

Dr. Latham gave notice that he should move the re- scinding of the resolution at the next meeting.

CEMETERY COMMITTEE,

The Cernetery Committee recommended that the wages of the Cemetery Keeper (Richard Anderson) be increased from 275. to 30s. per week. The minutes were approved. TECHNICAL INSTRUCTION COMMITTEE.

At a meeting of this committee the Clerk having re- ported the receipt of a cheque for £Bl from the County Council by the Treasurer to the Council, being the first instalment of the grant for Technical instruction pur- poses for the Session 1902-3, it was resolved that the Finance Committee be recommended to pass a cheque for £Bl in favour of the Viaduct Institute Technical Instruction Committee.

The minutes were approved. GAS COMMITTEE

THE LIGHTING OF ST. PETER'S CHURCH CLOCK.

A letter was read from Mr. J. Gooding, at a meeting of this committee, complaining of the defective light- ing of the Wesleyan Day Schools. It was resolved that the engineer be directed to visit the schools and report to the committee as to the lighting thereof. A letter was read from Mr. R. Barton with respect to the light- ing of St. Peter's Church clock. It was resolved that it appearing that the incandescent burners supplied by the Council for the lighting of the clock are un- suitable for the purpose ordinary burners be 'again supplied in lieu thereof. The committee further recommended that the street lamps in Fairclough- street be provided with incandescent burners for the better lighting of that street.

The Councir confirmed the minutes. THE WATER UNDERTAKING.

At a meeting the Water Committee the following reports of the Water Special Sub-Committee of the 30th October and Ist and 15th December, were read and adopted, viz.:—" The Clerk read acopy of a letter which he had addressed to the engineers calling their attention to the fact that there had been another breakdown of the pumps, and that as the con- tractor had not provided his pumps so that the Council could utilise the water from the new well, temporary supplies of water had to be obtained from the L. and N.-W, Railway Co.; also enquiring how the matter of the supply of the duplicate engines stood, as the committee would require this and other work to be proceeded with as soon as the Local Govern- ment Board sanctioned the scheme. He also read a letter received from the engineers,in which they stated that they were preparing specification for the engines. It was resolved that the action of the Clerk be approved and adopted. The engineer reported to the committee as to the breakdown of the pumps of No. 2 Borehole, and that it was necessary that the tubes should be taken up in order to remedy the defects. Resolved that the engineer be directed to obtain the requisite tackle for drawing the tubes, andalso to order stronger wheels for guides for the pump rods. Resolved that during the time the London and North-Western Railway Co.'s water is required by the Council to augment their sup- ply, such water be turned on at 4.30 each morning. The Clerk reported that Messrs. Gillespie and Mason, Ltd., had entered into an agreement for the supply of water to the Council, the engineer stating that the main would be coupled up next thy. A letter was read from Mr. G. IV. Coombs (Bridge-street), complaining of the unsatisfactory supply of water to his premises. The contractor's account, amounting to £5O, for work done in connection with the,proposed lowering of the bore- ' 'bole pumps was submitted to the committee, when it was resolved that the Clerk be instructed to return the account for particulars of the work done, to be fur- nished. It was resolved that the Clerk be instructed to write to the Local Government Beard with reference to the Council's application for their sanction to the bor- rowing of a sum of £18,900 for the purposes of the water undertaking (22,000 on account of the cost of laying distribution mains being sanctioned by them on the 27th September last),and in respect of which appli- cation an inquiry was held on the 31st October last, and ask them, in view of the urgency of the 'patter, to

sanction the borrowing of sums of £2,700 for new well and headings, and £2,330 for pumps and engines and extension Of engine-house." The Engineer having re- ported that the headings in the new well which had been sunk were now being driven in the red sandstone rock, it was resolved that a chemical analysis be made of the water coming therefrom for comparison with the water from the grey sandstone. The minutes were agreed to. THE PROPOSED ISOLATION HOSPITAL.

Ata meeting of the Highways and Sanitary Commit- tee it was resolved that the following report of the Isolation Hospital Sub-Committee of the Ist December be approved and adopted, viz. :—"December Ist, 1902. A letter was read from Lord Newton's agent, stating that his lordship is prepared to give to the Council one acre of land for the purpose of the erection of a hospital, and that should a larger area be required additional land could either be purchased or taken on lease for 999 years, and requesting to be informed of tie site of the proposed hospital. Resolved that the C.erk be instructed to inform Mr. Leslie that this com- mittee is of opinion that a site could be obtained either in the neighbourhood of Park-side road or Winwick- road, and that the committee would like to accompany them when visiting these localities to choose the site most suitable for the purpose." It was resolved that the Medical Officer of 'Health and the Inspector of Nuisances be directed to report to the committee with respect to the sanitary condition of Lyme-street. —A letter was read from Mr. J. Dunning, of 52, Legh- street, Earlestown, applying for the slaughter-house licence granted to Mr. George Wilde on the 3rd Novem- ber last to be transferred to him, he having taken over the premises from Mr. Wilde. It was resolved that the licence be transferred to Mr. J. Dunning.—At a special meeting Dr. Latham brought forward the question of the means to be adopted by the Council for isolating cases of small-pox,should an outbreak occur in this dis- trict,and after a discussion itwas resolved that the Clerk be instructed to write tothe Corporations of Leigh, St. Helens,Warrington,and Wigan,and enquire whether they would be able to admit cases of small-pox into thei infectious diseases hospitals should an outbreak occur in this district, and that should the Corporations above referred to,be unable to grant the desired accommoda- tion, the matter be then left to Drs. Latham and Patter- son to formulate a scheme for the provision of means of isolation in this district. The minutes were confirmed. GENERAL PURPOSES COMMITTEE. AN APPLICATION TO MR. CARNEGIE. THE RECENT RAILWAY FATALITY.

Dr. Latham having stated to the above Committee his views with respect to the establishment of a Public Library for the district,it was resolved that the Clerk be instructed to apply to Mr. Carnegie for the conditions under which he makes gifts for the provision of Public Libraries. It was resolved that the Clerk be instructed to again write to the general manager of the London and North-Western Railway Company, and point out to him the necessity of covering the present footbridges, and those proposed to be erected. at Earlestown passenger station. The Clerk submitted to the Com- mittee the Parliamentary notice, plan, etc., deposited by the Solicitor to the London and North-Western Rail- way Company with reference to the acquirement of land for improvements at the Efurlestown passenger station. A letter was read from the County Coroner together with copy of verdict and presentment (sent therewith) returned by the jury at an inquest held by him at Earles- town on the 3rd instant on the body of a lad who was killed whilst attempting to cross the Bridge Farm level crossing on the London and North-Western Railway, which stated that, in order to preserve life in the future, a bridge should be constructed by the Railway Com- pany for foot passengers, and requesting that the Coun- cil should communicate with the Company upon the subject. It was resolved that a copy of the verdict and presentment above referred to,be forwarded to the London and North-Western Railway Company, and that the Company be informed that the Council would feel obliged if they would do what the jury require. The Rev. J. C. Matthews, was granted permission to use the Market ground for the May Queen Festival, to be held on the last Saturday in May, 1903. The minutes were approved. THE SOUTH LANCASHIRE TRAMWAYS.

At a meeting of the Electric Supply and Tramways Committee the Clerk submitted proposed memorial to the Board of Trade, applying for an electric lighting provisional order for this district. It was resolved that the same be approved, and that the Council be recom- mended to affix its Common Seal thereto. The Clerk also submitted draft of the provisional order, which was approved by the committee. The Clerk submitted to the committee parliamentary notices, plans, &c.,deposited by the solicitor to the South Lancashire Tramways Company in relation t 3 the company's intended applica- tion to Parliament for additional powers. It was re- solved that the Clerk be instructed to return this Council as dissenting to the proposed application for an exten- sion of time for the completion of the tramways. The Surveyor was instructed to report to the committee with respect to the way in which the tramways have been laid by the Tramways Company in the neighbouring districts.

The minutes were agreed to. FIRE BRIGADE AND AMBULANCE COMMITTEE. At a meeting of this committee the Clerk reported that an advertisement had been published inviting tenders for the erection of the proposed fire engine house. It was resolved that the tenders, when received,be referred to the Chairman of this committee and the Chairman of the Finance Committee,with power to accept such tender as they may think best. Dr. Latham mentioned that the delay in regard to the ambulance was receiving the attention of the committee. The Chairman: It's about time it did. The minutes were adopted. APPLICATION FOR A LOAN.

At a meeting of the Finance Committee it was re- solved that application be made to the Public Works Loan Commissioners for the loan of .£13,175 to defray the cost of works in connection with the Councils water undertaking. The minutes were approved. EDUCATIONAL MATTERS.

The Clerk reported the receipt of a letter from the Clerk to the County Council asking the Council to appoint representatives to attend a conference with respect to the Education Act to be held at Prnton on Wednesday.

The Ch/i..irman and Mr. Borron were appointed to attend the conference on behalf of the Council.

COUNTY OF CHESTER. BYE-LAWS. For the good rule and government of the ADMINIS- MAUVE COUNIY OF CHESTER (excepting the Municipal Bizougha of CON GLETON. CREWE, DUKINKIELD. HYDE. MACCLEeFIELD, and STaLYBRIDGE situate therein). NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the following Bye-Laws were duly made at a Meeting oi the Cheshire County Council held at Chester Castle, on ,TEUReDAY, ti e 13th day of NOVEMBER, 1903, and navieg teen published as required by • the Statute in that behalf, and depesited with the Home Secretary tor the perioci of 40 days and not having been dire. alloweo, are now in force, viz. : PLACING OR DEPOSITING BROKEN GLASS &c., ON HIGHWAYS OR OTLER PUBLIC PLACES. NO person shall place, deperit, or leave any article of glass or any brokrn glass, or o7her sharp sub- stance, not being road material, on any highway or public place, in a position in which the same is likely to cause danger or anneyaece:to passengers or damage to property. HEDGE CUTTINGS, &c. No person shah ruff r any cuttings ot trees or hedges to remain upon any highway or public place, in a position in which tee same are likely to cause danger or anuoyauce to passengers or damage to property, for a longer period than one hour after sunset on the day upon which they shall have been cut. ANNOYANCE ON SUNDAY. No person on arty put Ito eoach or Gm( r public vehiclel for the eenveyance of passengers, or cn any coach, brake, waegonette, or other vehicle hired or used for tbe conveyance at pleasure parties and the like, shall on Sonoays blow any horn, or use any other noisy instrument, or mace, or combine with any other person or persors to make any loud singing or outcry while passing through any town, village, or hemlet, or by any Church, Chapel, or other place of Public Worship, to the annoyence or interruption of residents within aey inch town, village, or hamlet, or any persona worshipping in any such Church, Chapel, or ther place of Public Worship. Nothing in Vats Bye-Lsw shall prevent the use of a horn in a reasonable manner by one person oa a coach or sim.lar vehicle. PENALTIES. Any person who shall offend against any of the fore- going Byc-Laws shall be liable on summary convic- tion for each offence to a penalty nut exceeding forty shillings. REGINALD POTTS. Clerk oi the Cheshire County Council. Chester, let January, 1903.

CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH, NEWTON. The Young Men's Bible Class in connection with the above church held their first annual social on Saturday evening. The young men invited young ladies and about 140 sat down to tea, a number of lady members of the church presiding at the tables. After tea,games Sm., were indulged in. Mr. J. Isherwood contributed several songs and selections on the musical glasses. Mr. Ridyard gave a Lancashire sketch and Miss Bertha Smithassisted with several recitatioqs. Mr. Turbot sang "The green grass grew all round." During the evening a presenta- tion consisjng of a book was made by Mr. Owen to Mr Cook on his leaving town for Birmingham. The arrange- ments were efficiently carried out by Mr. Owen, of Newton.

To John Wilfred Delooze 30, Dudley-street. belongs the unenviable distinction Of being the first Warrington man to be placed upon the" block-list in accordance with the provisions of the New Licensing Act in re- gard to the treatment of habitual drunkards. Delooze aPPeared before Mr. F. Monks and other magistrates at tilLe County Borough Police Court on Monday, on the caarge of having been drunk and disorderly in Winwick- street at nine o'clock on Saturday evening. • P.C.Metcalfe stated that he found the prisoner lean- ing against the wall at the Central Station in a helpless condition. He took him into custody and he refused to Wat., lk• Witness obtained the assistance of P.C. Cooper v'6), and the prisoner, who made use of filthy language, wan taken to the Police Station.
